#79
The man before me looked nothing like his daughter.

Leah was small and petite, a cheerful ball of energy that was better left alone, a dumb puppy too happy for you to kick away.

This guy was a solid contender in the size and height department for my partial Oni form. Grey hair and beard shaved in style, arms thicker than trees, with a stature just as broad. A piercing gaze, face as if it was carved out of rock, from how intense it seemed, leaving no doubt about what his past profession was.

Definitely got into fights, enough to run a threat assessment on me the moment I walked in.

And right now, that man was glaring at me.

Not that I took it personally, he looked like someone who just glared at everything by default. Probably had a bad case of resting murder face, like Rachel.

"You the boy who saved my little girl?" he asked, voice deep.

"Daddy, I already told you he did!" Leah chimed in.

"And I'm asking him now." He spoke without looking at her.

"No," I replied flatly.

He stared. The way he held himself told me of military training. I had no doubt behind the counter, in arm's reach, was probably some kind of gun that he could whip out in a second before I took a step.

Good, I approve.

The bar was busy, too. By the time we reached here, the sun had gone down, and night had started. People were mingling about; various glasses were being knocked back after a day of living in this shitty world.

The bar was also located deeper into the seedier sides of the city, so I made sure to account for each person who came in. We were in the rougher end of Brockton, and it showed, everyone who walked in was strapped with either an arm or at least a small shiv. Not visible, but under my gaze, it wasn't difficult to find it.

I noted them all, then dismissed them. None was a threat.

"No?" Robert echoed my words.

"I didn't save them," I repeated, steady.

He looked at me, waiting for me to elaborate. But I had no idea what to elaborate on, so I just stayed silent and matched his stare. This silence stretched for some time.

"He doesn't talk much, Robert," Pritt said suddenly, tone dry as the desert. I only knew her name because Leah hadn't stopped talking since we left the alley.

The large man grunted, "You didn't save them, but you took care of them ABB thugs?"

"Yes."

"Can I ask why?" He looked puzzled.

I shrugged, "Needed to blow off some steam."

He considered my words, eyes closing as the mind worked. Then he nodded once, deliberate, satisfied enough from my answer.

"Where'd you learn to fight?"

"Self-taught."

"Any work experience?"

"None legitimate."

Robert looks me up and down and shows the instinct of someone who survived their fare share of skirmishes when he doesn't dismiss me as a threat.

I stay silent as he scrutinizes me since I'm doing the same.

"Whoa," Leah whispered. "This is so surreal."

"They do act alike, don't they?" Pritt murmured back, not even bothering to keep her voice down.

Robert and I both glare at them; unfortunately, all we got was amusement from them.

"We still need to talk about you girls walking alone." And their amusement died, both sharing winces at the reminder.

From the corner of the bar, one of the regulars — a burly guy halfway through his drink — chuckled. "Didn't think I'd live to see the day someone matched Bull Iron Robert."

His friend snorted. She had a half-shaved head with a nose piercing. "And by a kid, no less."

Both of them went pale the second Robert and I turned our synchronized death glares on them.

"Oh shit," one muttered, sinking into his chair. "There really are two of them."

Giving off a huff, the definitely retired soldier looked back at me, "You said you wanted a job."

"I did."

"Why should I give it to you?"

"Daddy!" Leah immediately jumped in, her hands flying to her hips like she was about to scold him. "You know he came here just because he heard you needed help!"

She said that proudly, as if I did something sweet instead of brutally assaulting six men, and any misplaced belief that I did this out of kindness needed to be crushed immediately.

"I came for the job," I say plainly. "I was planning on emotionally blackmailing your father into accepting me."

"Yeah, exactly, he came to help- huh?!" Leah gave me a betrayed look that did wonders to my soul. Her friend's shoulders shook as she tried to contain her laughter.

"Why would you need emotional blackmail?" Pritt managed to ask in between chuckles.

"I don't have any papers or experience. No one wanted to hire me." I pause, looking up to meet Robert's eyes, this damned teenage body making me to small for us to see eye to eye.

"Hmmm," The man closed his eyes.

When Robert opened them again, there was now an intensity to them, a sense of finality that made my muscles tense in readiness to spring into action. My senses narrowing and ignoring anything that would be a distraction in case a fight broke out.

"Why this job?" I heard the unasked question behind the simple words.

'Why a legitimate job?'

I already basically implied of doing criminal work, the fact that I'm also good enough to knock around six guys without a single scratch showed that I was proficient in what I did.

He clearly pegged me for what I was, someone who's seen blood, someone who doesn't flinch when things go from bad to worse.

If it were for money, there were easier, dirtier ways to gain it in this city.

Well, it doesn't matter anyway.

Because-

"I want to."

It was that simple.

I'm not doing this because I have to.

I'm doing it because I chose to.

That was all I needed.

Robert smiled, and the pressure building up for a fight melted away. That smile spoke of understanding, and I tried to ignore the weird feeling that coursed as he directed it to me.

"You're hired."

I couldn't help the smile that stretched across my face.

"Thank you for having me," I said, extending a hand. "Samuel."

"Robert Tress." He took my hand, shaking it firmly. His hand was calloused, the type I was familiar with, since my hands were the same.

Spoiler: Robert Tress

"Ah! So your name is Samuel!" Leah squealed, clapping her hands together. "That's such a beautiful name!"

Fuck, I forgot about her. I immediately regretted introducing myself.

"Oi! What's with that face?" Leah pouted, puffing her cheeks.

My feelings might have shone through, because Robert began to give a low chuckle, followed by Pritt who couldn't hold herself anymore, snickering as Leah whined.

How weird.

Why is it that seeing them laugh like this-

Makes everything feel so warm?

[FEAT: Acquire a legitimate job, even if through the power of emotional blackmail, which is incredible for a street rat who doesn't even know elementary subjects!

1x Silver Familiar Gacha Ticket]​


"Alright, given that you don't have any experience, pay close attention."

I stood behind the counter, holding the mug he'd handed me — apparently meant for a cappuccino — and watched carefully as he worked.

"When warming the milk, make sure to let it flow in a circle around the edge. It smooths out the foam and makes it easier to draw when making the coffee. Be careful not to touch the steam tube, it's piping hot, and you can easily hurt yourself."

As the milk began to froth and rise, he shifted aside, grabbing a mug that already had two shots of espresso waiting at the bottom.

"First, get rid of about a quarter of the milk, watch how I do it." He pours some of it into another smaller container. I notice how, by doing it slowly, the foam remains on the larger pitcher while the milk flows out cleanly into the smaller one.

"Now you don't have to worry about spilling anything by having too much milk while mixing the coffee." With a quick, practiced twist of his wrist, he poured the foam and drew a perfect white heart on top of the dark surface.

"…do it again."

Robert sighed, "Again?"

"C'mon, that's the third time you've said that!" Leah complained from her stool at the bar, legs swinging idly.

"The peanut gallery should stay silent."

"Hey!"

"She's got a point, you know," Pritt said without looking up from her phone, having gotten bored with this after my second request. "You're not gonna learn unless you actually try."

Yes, thank you, Pritt, for the obvious observation. I was just memorizing the fine details — the micro-adjustments of posture, the way his elbow moved with his wrist. Copying people was easier when you understood how they held themselves.

Also, a fun fact, phones apparently advanced a lot from what I remember. Of course, this world being five years in the future, I had expected some advancement on the technological front, but such a sudden spike of innovation was still difficult to wrap my head around.

"Fine," I finally said, nodding. "I should have enough."

Definitely not because of them egging me on.

Robert raised an eyebrow but handed me the tools. I poured out the excess milk and forced every muscle to move in perfect imitation — from the rotation of the wrist to the exact pressure on the cup.

You never realize how many tiny muscle groups you control until you try to move like a machine. It's hell.

Heat the milk. Smooth the foam. Wrist rotation. Arm movement. Draw.

The result: …less than impressive.

[FEAT: Prepare a mediocre cup of joe, good enough to drink but not enough to sell.

1x Bronze Random Gacha Ticket]​

I scowled at the wobbling excuse for a heart.

"Huh," Robert mused from behind me, "not bad for a first attempt."

"Not bad?!" Leah leaned over the counter, eyes wide. "How the heck did you even do that? I've been working here almost a year and I still can't do that!"

"Skill issue," I said flatly.

Her pout deepened instantly.

Pritt gave her a consoling pat on the shoulder, then tilted her head toward me. "She's right though. You sure you've never done this before?"

I shook my head.

She gave a low whistle. "Huh. When we tried it, we ended up spilling milk everywhere."

"Skill issue."

Pritt raised her phone like a weapon. "I will throw this at you."

I sniffed. "You'd miss."

Robert cleared his throat before the two could escalate, arms crossed as he studied me again. "You copied me," he said — not really a question.

"Yes."

"Is that why you asked me to do it three times?" Maybe I went a bit overboard, but I wanted my first attempt to be better than was expected. Give a bit of oomph in case Robert changed his mind about hiring me by proving how quickly I can learn.

"Yes," I said simply. "I needed to see how you moved — all of it. Legs, hips, shoulders, hands, fingers. Every detail."

Still bad however, the foam was off, the drawing was already dissolving, and the heart had been too fat to be proper.

Still, it was a start, even if it annoyed me a bit by how bad it looked.

"That's creepy, Sammy." Leah shuddered.

"The peanut gallery should stay silent," I said.

"Stop calling me that!"

"Don't call me nicknames."

"But mine are cute and yours aren't, so quit it!"

No, no, I don't think I will.

"Well, at least you know the basics of being a barista," Robert said. "Now you need to learn to prep the small menu items, know the drink names, and — most importantly…" He paused, looked me dead in the eye. "Take out the trash."

Classic. The more things change, the more they stay the same.

"Eh? Hey Dad, why'd you put that much emphasis on trash?" Leah asked, genuinely puzzled. I shouldn't have expected anything more from this ditz.

"Because it obviously isn't about trash, Leah." Pritt sighed at her friend.

"What? Then why did he say that?" She blinked.

I ignore the airhead and her friend, giving Robert a look that expresses my thoughts on the matter, just to make sure we're on the same page about what constitutes as 'trash'. I had already been kicked out once when I thought I understood what my boss wanted, but was very deeply wrong.

He nodded once, "Don't kill anyone."

Directives given. Mission parameters set.

"Kill? Hold up, wha-" Leah started.

"How much will I be paid?" I cut off the peanut gallery, ignoring the pitiful glare she sends my way.

Taking a napkin off the counter, he grabs a pen from his apron pocket and writes down a number.

"By doing all those services, about this much per month. That good?"

I look at what he wrote.

Were four zeros a lot per month? I curse my former self for never giving a shit about money, and as such, never giving me a frame of reference. A problem gained from having none as a kid, so it didn't matter, to having the gang take care of all my needs, so I never looked at the price of anything to get whatever I wanted.

I'd never learned what 'enough' actually looked like.

Still, given the choked gasp of Leah and Pritt, who were both looking over my shoulder, I guess it was pretty good.

I nod in agreement.

"When do I start?" I ask.

He opened his mouth to answer — and a sharp crash cut him off. The distinct sound of glass being shattered rang across the suddenly much livelier bar.

Two men, large and clearly intoxicated, were brawling, one was holding the remnants of a cup he used to smash into the other's face. They were shouting something at each other, but it was drowned by the sudden voices of the crowd, the mood shifting from subdued conversations to active entertainment.

But I ignored it all as my newest boss's words reached me.

"Right now."

That was all I needed to hear.

"Uhm, dude, what are you-?" Pritt tries to speak, managing to tear her gaze away from the sudden instance of violence, while Leah was still staring, even if she didn't seem surprised by the development.

I moved through the thinning crowd. People had already cleared from the area, having taken notice of the buildup before fists went flying, and were now just watching it play out. Some cheered on, others seemed indifferent.

It painted a picture of the bar, how this was frequent enough that nobody looked surprised. This kind of thing was apparently part of the evening's programming.

Whatever, my job is to throw away the trash, and that's what I'll do-

Oh, wait, Boss said I gotta open this up with diplomacy first.

I stepped between the two fighters and raised my hands, using the ornate politeness I'd picked up being shoved into social roles by the gang bosses. "Esteemed customers, please stop fighting and leave the premises."

I didn't understand half of the fancy words they used, but some could be picked up from the context.

The bar quieted down, all eyes now on me.

"The hell? What's a kid doing here?" One of the guys swayed as he spoke, face flushed from drinking, a hand bleeding from holding the broken glass.

The other snorted, "Get the fuck out of here, kid! Go back to sucking on yo momma's tits."

"I'll make you taste her tits!" And they were back to fighting, the one who took a cup to the face managing to tackle the other to the ground.

Some of the patrons cheered them on. The bar settled into an excited lull.

Very well.

In a second, they were separated.

"The hell?" one of them managed, blinking up at me.

I held them both by the scruff of their collars, firm enough that they couldn't pull free.

"Diplomacy has failed," I announced to the suddenly silent bar.

Then promptly turned around, walking to the front door and dragging the two troublemakers with me, their legs scrapping against the floor as I drag them towards the door.

"Hey, hey kid get off-" I pull him up and for a few seconds, his knees leave the floor, before I slam his face against the ground. He stopped moving.

"What the fuc—?!" the other started, and I didn't bother waiting. I drove my elbow into the top of his head hard enough to ragdoll him; his lights went out in a sick little clatter.

Eradication Fist truly was incredible, because in no reality, an elbow blow strong enough to knock someone out didn't mean heavy internal brain damage.

I opened the front door and threw them into the street. Close to the bar, someone would drag them home or at least make sure they didn't immediately get killed by something worse.

Wiping my hand like some proud janitor, I walked back into the still-stunned bar. Everyone watched with varying degrees of shock.

"Anyone else?" I asked.

Ah look at that, they're all back to normal.

People shuffled, then resumed their drinks and conversations as if nothing had happened.

[FEAT: Incite mass fear in a crowd of veterans without using any special abilities!
1x Random Gold Gacha Ticket]​

Ignoring the loitering masses, I make my way to my newest boss.

"Good job." Was his simple reply. Robert was also good at hiding his surprise, but I was even better at reading people, so it was noticeable.

Guess my threat level went up a few notches in his mind.

"Woooah! You were like, 'Leave now.'" I do not sound like that, Leah's light voice too different from my own, even as she deepened it.

Her voice was a high, dramatic commentary, all flailing hands and glittering eyes.

"And then they were like 'no way!'" Was she going to narrate the whole thing?

"And then you said, 'Diplomacy has failed.'" Again, I did not sound like.

"Hey, are you really self-taught?" Her scepticism was obvious, but at least it was good to know someone around here had basic common sense about what they see.

While I was pretty good at fighting before I died, it was secondary to my skill in killing. But after the upgrade of Eradication Fist, my fighting skill rose beyond comprehension.

Well, it's not a lie anyway, since I did technically learn it by myself, rolling the Gacha.

Leah stopped talking, both looking at me like I'm crazy.

Did I say something wrong? I thought such logic was obvious to everyone else. At least Robert nodded along with my words, seemingly already having a similar sentiment to that.

I don't know why Pritt reacted like that, however.

After all, she's the most dangerous person here.


"I'm home."

My voice echoed faintly through the apartment as I closed the door behind me, the words foreign, but they felt like they should be used in this situation.

The soft click of the door felt loud in the quiet. Only a few lights were on, one from the dining room, another faint glow bleeding in from the kitchen. The rest of the place was swallowed by shadows.

"You're late."

Rachel's voice came from the dining table. She was sitting there, elbows on the surface, chin resting on her hand. A single lamp above her cast long shadows across her face, leaving half of it in darkness. How long was she waiting for me there?

A brief glance at the clock hanging on the wall tells me it's almost one in the morning.

"I never told you when I'd be back," I pointed out, slipping off my shoes.

"It's very late."

"Sorry," I said simply, heading for the kitchen. "Next time I'll tell you."

The faucet creaked as I filled a glass of water. The sound of running water filled the silence for a moment. When I turned back, she wasn't glaring anymore, her expression had softened slightly, which I could generously call happy. A grunt escaped her, low and wordless, but I could read the pleased tone it contained.

"I got a job," I said, breaking the quiet.

She gave a short hum, which I interpret as questioning.

"Yeah, at a bar. I'll get paid by the end of the week, so I'll cover the rent then, if that's alright."

Her brows furrowed. "I didn't ask for it."

"I'm giving it anyway."

She blinked, visibly thrown off by that. "Why?"

I took a sip of water before answering. "Because I like you?"

I meant it in the way one might appreciate a quiet moment. She was blunt, didn't talk too much, and didn't pry, which I liked quite a bit. Apparently, that wasn't how she heard it.

Rachel froze. Then, slowly, color crept up her neck.

"…you're a fucking idiot," she muttered, turning away.

"You'll be a bigger idiot if you don't take it," I replied, deadpan.

Her eye twitched.

"Wanna go, fucker?" she snapped, stepping forward and grabbing me by the shirt. She pulled me closer, her grip firm enough to wrinkle the fabric.

I leaned forward until we were almost nose to nose. Her breath hitched, but she didn't back off. Her gaze dipped — just briefly — to my lips before darting back up.

"No," I said simply. "That's why I'm paying. To stay here."

We stayed like that for a moment, caught in some strange middle ground between tension and the fact that I didn't want to move and damage my shirt; she had a very tight grip.

Then she shoved me back, muttering curses under her breath.

"Fine, whatever! Go shower again, you stink!" My, what harsh words.

Still, I frowned slightly. I was fairly sure my Musk ability was supposed to make my scent appealing; apparently, it just made me smell stronger. Wonderful.

Rachel paused in the middle of the hallway, still red in the face.

"And leave the clothes out!" she called back. "I'm washing later."

Spoiler: Blushing Rachel

"Sure," I said.

Her door slammed shut a moment later. The lamp flickered once before going out, leaving the dining room dim again.

I finished my water in silence, staring into the empty glass.

"Sit."

"Woof."

"Paw."

"Woof?"

"…bang."

The little puppy immediately flopped onto his side, legs sticking out like a fallen soldier.

I take this as an absolute win.

A second later, he sprang back up, tail wagging furiously as he ran circles around my legs, occasionally tugging at the hem of my pants as if demanding more attention, more praise, more whatever-it-is that made his tiny dog brain happy.

How did I get roped into this?

Excellent question.

The answer: Rachel asked if I wanted to walk the dogs with her, and I said yes.

Three days had passed since I moved in with her. Friday morning rolled in cold and misty, sea fog hugging the ground the way only a harbor town like Brockton Bay could manage. The streets were damp, the air smelled faintly of salt and diesel, and the leash in my hand was already wet from the dog's enthusiasm.

In that time, I'd fallen into a routine.

Wake up early.

Train this weaker teenage body back into something respectable.

Handle small errands that I only now realized existed, apparently, humans need things like detergent, toilet paper, and dish sponges. Leah had been unexpectedly helpful with that list. I would never tell her that.

Then I'd head to work from noon until night, learning the subtleties of being a barista while also making sure nobody got any funny ideas while I was on the clock. As per the words of my newest boss, I am to crack down on any type of aggressive behaviour with extreme prejudice.

Surprisingly, there had been no pushback from the ABB so far. I half-expected them to come looking for me, but according to Robert, the guys I beat up probably didn't tell a soul. Shame does wonders to people's ability to keep their mouths shut.

On the second day, I also received this:

[FEAT: Became a known strong entity in the eyes of the powers of Brockton Bay
1x Random advantage Gold Gacha Ticket]​

Which meant the authorities had finally taken note of Ura-doji.

If Brockton Bay worked anything like Salvador, every gang had someone sitting comfortably inside the PRT, leaking whatever intel came through the official channels. So if the PRT knew, the gangs knew.

Well, except the Empire. They were very likely the ones who spread the information in the first place.

But honestly? I couldn't care less about their little information circles. My attention was on my new job. It bothered me at first that I wasn't paid per fight — I mean per job — but then I remembered this was a normal job. You work a set number of hours, on set days, and get paid based on total hours. A strange concept for someone who previously got paid the moment he dragged a problem out of a building by the throat.

Annoyingly, this also meant I couldn't give Rachel the rent money for another four days.

Normally I would be browsing the markets right now, searching for ingredients to improve my cooking. But when Rachel asked me to join her for the morning dog walk — both for company and to help wrangle any overly aggressive strays — I agreed. Those were the terms for me staying at her place, after all. She seemed pleased. I've learned quickly that Rachel values people who follow through their word.

Fortunately, the dogs were being agreeable today. No fights, no sudden lunges at birds, no escaping into traffic. Miraculous, really.

Which left me here: standing in the middle of a foggy sidewalk, staring down at a three-legged puppy gazing up at me with the biggest, most cosmic eyes I'd ever seen.

I had been trying to teach him tricks for the past two hours.

There had been zero success.

"Still trying?"

"Don't make fun of me," I mutter.

"I will."

"Fuck you."

Rachel snorted, the corner of her mouth twitching upward for half a second before her expression fell back to her usual glower. The fog curled around us as we stood there, watching the dogs roughhouse in the wet grass.

"Are you going to save more?" I ask.

"Yes," she said seriously. Her face was neutral, but her shoulders were set in that stubborn, immovable way of hers.

"You won't be able to save all of them," I said.

The effect was immediate. Her jaw tightened, mad that I brought it up, but didn't seem to direct the anger at me specifically, just angry at the words.

"I know," she snapped. "But that shit doesn't matter. I'll save as many as I can."

"And then what?" I pressed. "Where will you put them? You need somewhere. You won't be able to take care of every dog you save by just putting them on your backyard."

"Shut up!" This time the glare did aim directly at me, in both anger and beneath it a layer of hurt, as if I was putting down her goal of trying to get as many as possible.

"Okay," I said simply. "Then I'll think of something."

She blinked. "…Huh?"

"You need somewhere to put everyone," I said, nodding at the pack currently tackling each other in the mud. They were brighter now, more alive than the starved, trembling creatures I'd seen in that basement. "So, I'll think of something. Maybe buy a dog shelter."

That would take a ridiculous amount of money. But the Gacha had already spat out stranger miracles. And if it didn't give me what I needed, then I'll find another way.

No," Rachel said immediately, the word spat with the kind of disgust people reserve for rotten meat. "Shelters are shit."

I can understand her having problems with dog shelters, since most charities barely receive any money or resources needed for their stated goal; most of the time, the parties in charge of the charity keep the largest part.

I'm guessing that Rachel met a few shelters that didn't really care about the dogs they were advertising as caring. While I don't think all of them are the same, I'm confident enough to say that most are just from seeing the state of the city.

"What if you're the one in charge?" I asked. Her head snapped toward me, the interest in it was obvious, as much as it was the hope trying and failing to disguise itself within her eyes.

"You know how to take care of dogs," I continued. "You'd run it the right way, and people could bring dogs to you. You'd have a place to keep them. On top of everything, you'll have an actual government-supported business, so it should all be legal."


The more I said it, the more the idea took shape. Of course, we'd need licenses, paperwork saying we're qualified, money for food, medicine, space, repairs…

But it wasn't impossible.

Rachel stayed quiet, staring at the dogs but clearly thinking, brow slightly furrowed. Her anger had cooled into contemplation.

Some might question why I'm going so far for a girl that I've only known for all of three days. And I would normally agree with them, if it weren't for the fact that I am now conceptually bound to find myself in the narrative of the world.

For you see, if I do indeed give Rachel a dog shelter, that would have long-standing consequences that would create waves, disrupting the normal path that this world would take, right?

As such, it would constantly feed into my Curse of Interesting Times, right?

Man, I'm so smart that sometimes I scare myself.

I let Rachel mull over the conversation in silence and turn my attention back to the furry menace chewing on my ankle.

"Hand."

"Woof!"

The puppy licked me.

"No, hand."

Another lick. Slobberier.

While I'm debating how far I could reasonably throw this tiny gremlin, I catch movement beside me.

"You're not doing it right." Rachel, who was watching in amusement, decided to step in, plopping herself down next to me on the cold grass of the park.

"He doesn't understand what you want from him," she says, nodding at the puppy struggling with his own back legs. "And you're not giving him a reason to care."

I stare at the dumb puppy, idly noting that he was a boy by her words, "How do I do it, then?"

She paused, scowl returning as she thought, "Rumble when you speak," she said, demonstrating it with a soft growl at the back of her throat that sent her voice into a low pitch.

It was strange, but the puppy's reaction was immediate, his attention snapping toward her like she had just flipped a switch.

"Also, stiffen up," she adds. "Being loose like that isn't helping anything."

I make myself become rigid. I don't like it. Every part of me wants to stay loose and coiled, ready in case something happens, but I force the springiness out of my shoulders anyway. It feels unnatural, like I'm locking the safety pin of a gun.

"Be firm about it," she finishes. Then she turns to the puppy, gives a sharp, low command, "Roll!" and flicks her wrist in a clean sweeping motion.

The puppy watches her hand. I can physically feel the cogs in that empty box work to make a connection, then awkwardly rolls once and pops up again, looking ridiculously proud of himself.

"You're amazing," I say before I think about it. Was it even possible for a human being to understand dogs at such a level that they would follow your commands? For a second, I wondered if it had to do with her power, but the words of the book rejected the notion, stating firmly that it was a skill earned from years of living with them and understanding them in ways normal people don't bother learning.

Rachel seemed thrown off by my statement, frowning harder my way, even as a dust of red showed that she was flustered by the compliment.

"Tsk. You try it," she mutters, yanking her attention toward the other dogs. She tries to look focused on them, though I can feel her sneaking glances at me when I go back to focusing on her words.

I look down at the puppy again.

Alright. Strong voice. Clear intent. And a motion that actually matches what I want. I try to put everything she said into the way I stand, the way I breathe. A set of languages spoken through our bodies to achieve a means of nonverbal communication, with a small help from commands.

There's a strange sensation in my chest, almost like something instinctive unfurling, settling into place.

My inner Oni stirs.

"Hand."

Something warm presses against my palm.

Not the puppy's paw.

I blink and slowly raise my eyes. Rachel is staring right back at me, equally wide-eyed, her hand resting on mine like she didn't even think before doing it.

We hold there for a second.

Then she snatches her hand away so fast she nearly trips over herself, face flaring bright red. She bares her teeth in something that looks like a threat but feels more like panic, then spins on her heel and marches off without a word.

I look down at the puppy.

"You got any idea what that was?"

"Woof."

#137
The house was quiet in that way it only got at night, when the pups were too tired to cause trouble, and the city outside stopped growling. I set the pan back on the stove, the smell of chicken and garlic rising with the heat. Fried rice wasn't complicated, but I'd gotten better at it—enough that the colors looked right and the pieces sat in the pan exactly how I wanted them. Going from not knowing how to boil water to this felt like a huge leap, even if in the grand scheme, it wasn't much.

One of the pups nosed against my ankle, sniffing for fallen scraps. I nudged him away with a foot before he could test his luck. Rachel loved them, but even she didn't want them sick from eating onions or anything else in here.

The back door creaked. Rachel stepped in from the yard, boots muddy, shoulders squared like she'd been arguing with the world again. She stopped the moment she saw me standing at the stove.

"I thought you worked until midnight," she said. Her voice wasn't accusing, just surprised, thought Rachel was also sharing the tone he now knew as 'caught with your hand on the cookie jar.'

"Boss let me off early," I answered, tapping the pan to keep the rice from sticking. "Dinner'll be ready soon."

Nothing else came from her. Just quiet, which of itself wasn't strange. It was strange, however, how I could hear her fidgeting in place.

When the silence stretched long enough for the chicken to finish browning, I finally turned. She stood halfway between the door and the kitchen table, jaw tight, hands at her sides. It was the look of someone bracing for impact.

"Rachel?"

She exhaled once, sharply. "We won't be able to eat together tonight." The way she said it felt like she didn't know why it bothered her. "Job came up. Don't know when I'll be back."

I held her gaze. "Will you be in danger?"

That made her bristle, chin lifting the way it always did when pride jabbed at her ribs. "I can take care of myself."

"I know you can," I said evenly. "But that isn't what I asked."

Her shoulders twitched. I could see the anger she was fighting against to answer me properly, and I felt happy to see she would restrain herself just to answer me. "Yes," she muttered.

Maybe I should sound commanding more often? Rachel seemed to follow my words if I did, even If she turned red in anger.

I closed my eyes for a breath, weighing the words that came next, then opened them again. "You're strong. You'll come back."

She answered with a quiet noise that wasn't quite agreement but wasn't disagreement either, then turned away with quick steps, obviously creating distance, believing the conversation to be over.

I watched her disappear upstairs before turning back to the stove. The pups followed her halfway, yipping for attention that was given in the form of quick scritches, then circled back to me, hoping something had fallen while they weren't looking. I shifted them away again with my leg, finished seasoning the rice, and packed part of it into a container before it cooled too much.

Rachel's footsteps returned, heavier now with the gear she'd strapped on. She came down in her costume, face unreadable until she noticed the bag on the counter. Inside lay the container—rice in one section, chicken in another, and vegetables in a third.

She stared like it was about to grow teeth and jump her.

"What's that?" she asked.

"It's for you."

"For me?" The confusion in her tone tugged at something old in my memory, someone handing me food when I had nothing, asking the same question because I couldn't imagine receiving something without debt attached.

Granted, it had been attached at the time, tying me further into the gang, but I wouldn't learn that for years to come.

I nudged the bag toward her. "Hunger is the enemy. Eat to fight it, and grow strong from it."

Rachel didn't look at me, not directly. Her fingers brushed the handle of the bag like she wasn't sure she was allowed to touch it.

It was quite funny. Bitch was a wanted criminal, with murders under her activities, a feral, rough-edged girl who commanded monsters, and here she was, unsure of how to react to a gesture she didn't know how to process.

"…Why?" she finally asked.

I didn't answer right away. My gaze dropped to her hands, scarred knuckles, dirt under the nails from playing and training with her dogs, yet her hands were steadier than most men I'd fought. Rachel was a girl who spent all of her money on her dogs, almost reaching the point of neglecting herself for them.

Rachel was a girl who knew hunger.

Hunger had played a large part in my childhood. Hunger for food drove me to steal, hunger for safety drove me to strength, and hunger for challenge drove me through more battles than I can count.

Hunger was an old ghost. Most people didn't understand what it meant to grow up counting the days between meals and pretending the ache was normal. I didn't realize until I was older that it wasn't something you were supposed to live with, that you could simply eat until the pain stopped.

I walked towards Rachel, ignoring her question.

So, at least –

"And this," Within his hands, a card appeared, beautifully silvered, within it the picture of a great wolf, fur dark as night, flames seeping through its maws, "is a gift."

There was no need to think about which card I wished to give her, after all, it's almost as if the card itself was pulsating with happiness at the thought of connecting with Rachel.

…are Install Cards sentient?

Bah, who cares?

The air rippled. A thin glow rose from my palm, gathering into form. Metal solidified out of nothing, silvered edges, a polished face, and an illustration of a wolf bigger then the trees surrounding it, bigger than any real creature had the right to be. Its fur was pitch-dark, flames licking out from between its teeth.

Rachel seemed mesmerized by it. I could see in her eyes as they brightened. The light around the card matched such brightness, then burst into a soft scatter of sparks. Dozens—maybe hundreds—of small motes circled her in slow orbits. They brushed against her skin, sank through her clothes, and vanished into her like she was drinking them in.

She inhaled sharply, hands flexing at her sides.

When the last ember faded, she looked down at herself, then at me. "What was that?"

"My power," I said. "It's more than what you've seen so far. I guess you can say I can also transform other people into monsters if they wish," I leaned back against the counter, watching her reaction, "If you're ever in danger, call the name of the card. It'll answer you, allowing you to tap the power contained within it."

Rachel blinked. Once. Twice. She looked stunned in a way I hadn't seen before, thrown completely off-balance.

"Why?" she whispered.

I opened my mouth, ready to give her an easy line—some half-truth. "…Well, you gave me a place to—"

"Bullshit." Her voice cracked like a whip. She stepped closer, eyes sharp, breathing just a little too fast. "Letting you crash here isn't the same as—this." She jabbed a finger toward the container of food. "Or cooking for me." Another jab. "Or walking with the dogs and me." A third jab, right to my chest. "Or giving me… whatever the hell that was!"

Her face flushed with frustration, but her pupils were blown wide with something else. Heat. Confusion. Want. She didn't even seem to realize it, and I could barely understand the surge of emotion fluttering through her eyes.

"Tell me why you're doing all of this," she demanded.

I didn't answer right away. Her question punched deeper than it should have. She wasn't wrong. Everything she listed was true. I didn't have to do any of it; hell I started doing this in hopes of using her exposure to feed my Curse.

It wasn't like me to get entangled, to open doors that didn't need opening.

So why her?

Why this girl I'd known for less than two weeks?

I searched inside for logic, but found myself running into a great lack of it. So I reached a new conclusion, one I couldn't help but internally scoff at even as it presented itself to be the only choice.

"…We are the same," I said at last.

Rachel's breath caught.

"We both had shitty lives," I continued. "We both clawed our way out. Got stronger because of it." My gaze drifted to the knife on the cutting board, still slick with the sheen of chicken fat. It reminded me of the blades I'd used for far worse things.

"And somewhere along the way… I guess I started thinking of you as a friend."

Heat crawled up my neck. I didn't blush often, but the admission felt like saying something dangerous out loud, like naming a weakness.

Maybe I'd been using the Curse of Interesting Times as an excuse—pretending my choices weren't choices. Pretending this wasn't something I wanted.

To care is to risk loss.

To love anything is to open a wound.

But even knowing that, I'd stepped closer anyway to her. Am I that starved for any kind of relationship?

{…}​

…that's rhetorical, don't answer that.

Risking a glance at my landlady, I witness as Rachel stared like she wanted to lunge at me, Something flashing through her face that I didn't know how to read, I wrote it off as embarrassment and looked away to give her space.

She cleared her throat, voice lower than before. "What's it named?"

It took me a second to understand. "The card?"

She nodded once.

A small smile tugged at my mouth. The name was fitting, almost obvious; it was thus perfect for her.

"Hellhound."

Rachel snorted.

The mall was louder than I expected. There was bright light filtering through high glass panels, overlapping conversations bouncing off each other, creating a static of sound, and the sheer amount of people going about their days, creating a veritable flood of people. It was very different than Salvador; people moved with tension in their shoulders and knives hidden in their pockets, always staying wary of anyone who came too close. Here, despite Brockton Bay having parahumans who could level buildings, people walked casually, drifting from storefront to storefront like they had forgotten what real danger tasted like.

Or maybe Brocktonites were just good at ignoring dangers.

A man sitting in a public bench tapping his phone, broad shoulders and calloused fingers, it spoke of hard labour, if he were to lunge, the safest angle to break his knee would be from his right side. Here, though, with superpowers in the mix, the divide felt infinitely smaller. Superpowers obviously did not care about gender or muscle mass. Huh, halfway through observing everything, I realised something else: I was finally used to having two eyes again. Depth perception no longer felt like guessing. My steps landed exactly where I intended. My hands moved with precision I hadn't felt in years. It was comforting to reacquire a weapon you thought you'd lost forever.

If there is one thing I'm grateful for when returning to the age of fifteen, it is gaining my left eye back. It had been a bitch and a half to learn how to live with only one and double the trouble to compensate for it in the field.

Fashion, unfortunately, was a subject I had less experience with than quantum physics. I stared at mannequins wearing combinations of fabric that I didn't understand. Why the hell would I wear a black sweater over a white shirt? And there were so many styles highlighted by the boards to indicate where each article could be found.

As she should, because I don't think I have seen someone as beautiful as her before. Full lips, gleaming blue eyes, a beautiful cascading river of gold for hair, with hips and legs for days, while also sporting a bosom to make wives cry in envy.

Back in Salvador, she would definitely be regarded as a prostitute walking around with that little clothing.

Hmmm, the words within my soul seemed to grasp at threads of recognition, but they didn't have enough information to work with; that was all I needed to know that the girl before me was too trouble for what she was worth.

And then it clicked.

I hate this fucking curse; can I not go out a single time without bumping into bullshit?

Force Field Generator, invisible and extends a few millimetres over skin and clothes. It can block almost anything within the setting. #251
Amelia Dallon POV:

Panacea should be in the hospital right now.

The thought doesn't arrive suddenly; it never does. It settles in the back of Amy's mind the moment she stops moving, the moment her hands aren't working, and someone isn't thanking her with watery eyes and shaking hands. It presses down gently at first, then harder, until it becomes a weight she carries with her everywhere.

Every second she stands here is another second someone else is suffering. Every laugh, every breath taken without the sterile smell of antiseptic in her nose, feels borrowed from someone who needs it more. Someone dying quietly in a room she isn't in.

She watches people drift past the storefronts, arms full of shopping bags, friends tugging one another toward sales and displays, laughing over clothes they'll wear once or twice.

None of them look like they're counting seconds the way Amy always does.

It makes her chest tighten with envy.

The guilt of using free time reaches the familiar spiral, one that she is familiar with: why am I even in school? Sitting through lectures she'll never use, memorizing things that have nothing to do with the only thing she's actually good for. Healing isn't a career path so much as a sentence, and every attempt to imagine herself doing anything else ends the same way, with guilt crushing her lungs until breathing feels optional.

If she ever tried to hold a normal job, really tried, Amy is certain it would destroy her. She wouldn't last a week without feeling like she was murdering someone by clocking in.

It's Sunday. Normally, she would already be in the ER by now, handling the weekend casualties, the drunk drivers, the fights, the accidents that pile up whenever people decide rules don't apply to them for two days. If not that, then the medical ward, where the beds never seem to empty no matter how many she clears.

She would stay until the staff decided to kick her out.

The pressure swells again, nausea curling low in her stomach, cold sweat prickling along her spine. Her body knows exactly where she should be, and it hates her for not being there.

Amy forces herself to breathe slowly, quietly, anchoring herself to the moment. Focusing in the sound of the people milling around the shop the ventilation running.

Push down the guilt, so she can actually live in the moment.

Normally, no one could pull her away from the hospital like this. Anyone else would've been brushed off, ignored, or guilted into leaving her alone.

Except Victoria.

Her sister had dragged her here with a grin and a promise of 'just an hour' – which had been almost three hours ago at this point, her aching feet reminded her - and Amy hadn't had the strength to say no. She never does, not to Vicky. The nausea shifts, turning inward now, aimed squarely at herself.

She's disgusting.

The guilt of not healing is quieter when she's with her sister, softened by the excuse that this is time spent with the one person she's allowed to prioritize. And that relief only feeds the deeper rot twisting in her chest, because Amy knows exactly why it works.

If she closes her eyes for even a second, it almost feels like they're—

"He's hot."

Amy blinks hard, her thoughts slamming to a stop as if someone yanked the cord out of her head.

"What?"

Vicky's leaning casually against the wall near the changing booths, arms crossed, eyes very obviously fixed on the curtain Samuel disappeared behind. The mall lights catch in her blonde hair, making her look effortlessly put together in that way that has Amy mesmerized.

"C'mon," Vicky says, smirking, "don't give me that. You were looking too."

Amy feels heat rush to her face before she can stop it, sharp and unwelcome.

"No, I wasn't."

Her sister snorts. "Girl, you were practically drooling."

Amy huffs and rolls her eyes, crossing her arms even as she reflexively glances toward the booth. She absolutely had not been staring. That would be ridiculous.

"He's not that good-looking," she insists, the words coming out faster than she intends.

Vicky looks delighted by this. "Really? You're telling me you wouldn't want to feel how strong those muscles are?"

She punctuates it with an exaggerated eyebrow wiggle, clearly enjoying herself far too much.

She scrambles for a distraction. "Aren't you back with Dean?"

Vicky waves it off without even looking away from the booth. "It's okay to look at the menu if you eat at home."

Amy stares at her.

Deadpan.

Vicky catches the look, and laughs, her voice beautiful.

"I can still hear you!"

Amy sputters as Samuel's voice carries easily out of the changing booth, casual and unbothered, as if he isn't currently broadcasting to half the store.

"Good!" Vicky shoots back unbothered, "then maybe you'll tell us who your favourite dancer is."

Amy becomes acutely aware of the way heads turn, curiosity prickling at her skin. A few people pause a little too long, eyes flicking between her and Victoria with that particular sharpness that comes from recognition. Being out of costume helps, and the carefully chosen accessories do their job well enough, but the second-hand embarrassment still hits her like a wave.

God, this better not find itself in PHO.

"Well," Samuel continues, "she was blonde, had blue eyes, and called herself Glory Girl."

"Bitch, you wanna go?" Vicky snaps instantly.

"No thanks," he replies, deadpan. "Already have a 'dancer' for that."

Amy winces, half from the attention and half from the sheer audacity of it.

Normally—normally—someone talking to her sister like that would make her bristle on instinct. She's spent years watching people orbit Victoria with awe, fear, or thinly veiled worship, and she's learned to be protective in the quiet ways Vicky never notices.

But coming from Samuel, it just… doesn't land that way.

Instead of anger, Amy feels a reluctant flicker of amusement. Samuel doesn't soften his tone, and he certainly doesn't defer. He treats Victoria like a person, and that alone puts him in a category most people never reach.

People love Glory Girl, loved the symbol of Glory Girl. Amy understands why—she's bright with her smile and carries an air of unshakable confidence that makes her impossible to ignore. But that love comes with distance, and it means that very few people ever push back against Vicky's wishes. Watching Samuel do it so effortlessly makes something twist in Amy's chest.

Jealousy, she realizes faintly.

Vicky looks almost happy as they bicker, energized in a way Amy doesn't see often. It's new for her, someone who doesn't immediately fold or fawn, and Amy can tell she's enjoying the novelty.

Her thoughts drift as the argument continues, half-listening, half spiraling. She remembers how she'd first clocked Samuel as a problem—another fan, maybe, someone who'd latched onto her sister through sheer persistence. It wouldn't be the first time. Years of public attention have trained them to be cautious, but it was still difficult to sort genuine people from those chasing proximity to fame.

She had been sure he was the latter.

Sure, that he was manipulating the situation, intruding on what was supposed to be a rare, normal day with her sister, that he'd walked off with Vicky, casual as anything, leaving Amy behind.

The spike of anger she'd felt then still burns when she remembers it, sharp and sudden—and how quickly it had slipped through her fingers the moment he spoke. The way he'd looked at her, said lover's spat like it was obvious.

Nothing has ever terrified her more.

One glance. That's all it took.

Samuel had taken one look at her and seen it. As if her thoughts were written on her face for his eyes to see.

The realization had frozen her in place, dread swelling until it pressed against her ribs. The fear wasn't just that he knew—it was that he could say something. That he could turn to Vicky, casual and blunt, and tear Amy's life open with a sentence.

The thought still makes her stomach lurch.

And then—

Amy swallows hard, pushing down the flush.

Apparently, there are no depths to her depravity.

Because tangled up with that fear is something else. A heat unlike anything she ever felt, the way her body stiffened and trembled with need. How the thought of him holding that secret over her, of pushing her, of forcing himself at her…

He walked pass her, giving her a piercing glance and-

Well, let's just say that there was a reason why Amy also bought a new pair of panties.

This never happened before! Amy was one hundred percent sure that she's gay. She's always been gay. There isn't a single memory she can point to that contradicts that.

Except that maybe she just had a very specific type? One that apparently Samuel hit all the check list for.

And of course, those weird feelings that cropped up in response to Samuel looking at her too long, whenever he spoke directly, or at that piercing gaze of his.

Amy will be the first to say that her search history wasn't the purest, but up until now the wildest things she searched up was some light bondage.

Who knew that she had a thing for blackmailing?

Not her, that's for fucking sure!

Still… there's a quiet, relief buried underneath the panic. The knowledge that, for once, her feelings aren't circling back to the one person they're not allowed to touch.

Unfortunately, Amy would like to never again see Samuel in her life, just like he said he would disappear after Vicky was done with him. Not only had he been scary sharp at seeing through her feelings, but these heart spikes also weren't good for her health.

She's just thankful he doesn't have a phone, since it meant Vicky wouldn't be able to drag a means of communication.

That was a load off her mind, even as traitorous as it is, a small part of that same mind echoes Vicky's earlier sentiment.

He is annoyingly hot.

"Man," Vicky mutters, arms crossed now, watching the curtain like it personally offended her, "this guy has no manners. No idea how to treat a lady."

Her voice pulls Amy back into the present. The bickering has stopped.

"It feels weird," Amy admitted at last, breaking the lull while Samuel remained hidden behind the thin curtain of the changing booth.

"Right?!" Vicky snapped her fingers in immediate agreement, clearly relieved she wasn't the only one thinking it. "Even if he didn't know who we were, you'd think he'd at least be a little excited about meeting capes. But he didn't even blink." She tilted her head toward the booth, lowering her voice only slightly. "It's like it didn't matter for him."

Amy followed her sister's gaze and found herself nodding despite herself. Vicky wasn't wrong. Even after learning they were part of New Wave, a group of superheroes established in Brockton Bay, and just the fact that they were parahumans, Samuel hadn't reacted beyond an interested hum.

He just held that same unreadable, flat expression that was annoyingly attractive.

No, not attractive at all! In fact, Amy couldn't wait to see what lay beyond that piece of wood he called a face.

Then again, Amy had known him for maybe half an hour, and she was already convinced very little made that blank face crack.

Still…

"Do you think he's met capes before?" Amy asked quietly. It would explain it, a few of their regulars in fan meetings are much more relaxed than newcomers.

"Do—" Vicky cut herself off mid-sentence, licking her lips as her eyes flicked back toward the booth. She leaned closer, voice dropping into a conspiratorial whisper. "Do you think it has something to do with his scars?"

Amy found her face falling into a worried frown.

Yeah. That.

It wasn't just his fit body that had caught their attention earlier. It was the scars, dozens of them, of various sizes and uneven, crossing his skin in ways that spoke of violence. Some were old enough to have faded into pale lines, others still angry and newer, months at most if Amy's numbers were right.

Working in the ER as Panacea had trained her eye whether she liked it or not. She knew the difference between accidents and intent, between survival and damage endured. And while she hated judging people by appearances—especially when, beneath her hands, everyone became the same fragile collection of tissue and blood—it would be a lie to say her first thought upon seeing Samuel hadn't been thug.

This guy looked like someone who got into fights- No, more like someone who expected them. Tall and coiled, posture strong in a way that dared anyone to try something, chin lifted, implying he was already confident about how it would end. Like he knew he'd be the one walking away.

Fucking Hot.

Not the point!

"Eeh?" Vicky's brows shot up, intrigue lighting her face. "Then you don't think—"

She was getting loud again.

"Shhh," Amy hissed, grabbing her sleeve and tugging her closer. The proximity made something warm bloom in her chest, a strange, unwelcome flutter as they leaned in together to whisper. "Look, he obviously didn't have a great life, but that doesn't mean we should treat him differently." She paused, then fixed Vicky with a pointed look. "And we definitely shouldn't ask insensitive questions."

Brazil.

The word alone carried weight in Amy's mind, heavy with context she couldn't ignore. The largest country in South America, and statistically the one with the highest concentration of villainous capes in the Americas, second only to China worldwide.

The comparison with the United States was always stark. Here, the PRT and Protectorate had managed, more or less, to fold capes into a structured system of authority, turning superpowers into a regulated branch of government.

South America hadn't been so lucky. Power there had fractured into something closer to feudalism, with the strongest ruling from the top while gangs carved cities into territories, governments left standing mostly as decorative shells to preserve the illusion of order.

Corruption wasn't a flaw in the system; it was the system.

And Samuel had lived there.

From what little she'd seen, he hadn't just survived it, he'd been worn down and hardened in equal measure. The scars across his body told a story Amy didn't need words for, one of repeated violence and endurance, of damage taken and lived through rather than avoided.

She realized, distantly, that she hadn't been wrong to be afraid of him. Not because he might be a gang member, not because he was hostile or cruel, but because anyone who could live through that environment and come out the other side marked like that was, by definition, dangerous. Even without intent, even without malice, experience like that left an imprint.

Just existing in a world that could hurt you that deeply was enough to make someone sharp around the edges.

There was something wrong with her, Amy realised.

Looking away from her sister, she once again tried to calm her thudding heart, and desperately wondered when they could go home so she can change into a new pair of panties.

Was she really so damn thirsty?!

Dammit, maybe letting off a bit of steam at home wouldn't be so bad?

No, no!

Ugh, this was all his fault.

Dating meant time, and time meant hours away from the hospital, away from patients who didn't get to choose whether they were hurt or sick or dying. It meant prioritizing someone else when she already struggled to justify prioritizing herself at all, and that alone made her chest feel tight with guilt.

It wouldn't be fair to anyone, not when she knew she would always choose the hospital first, not when she wouldn't compromise, couldn't compromise, without hating herself for it. And worse than that was the thought of rejection, of opening herself up and being told no, of feeling small and unwanted when she already carried so much self-loathing it felt like a second skin.

There was another fear she never said out loud, even to herself: the quiet terror that if she ever got into a real emotional fight with someone she cared about, something might snap, that her power might slip in a moment of hurt or anger and she'd do something she could never take back.

Samuel Rodriguez POV:

I got back home early today, but that was because I also started work much earlier today.

Hmm, the house is still dark, with every light still off, so Rachel hasn't come back from her work yet. She warned me she'd be late today, vague as always and without giving a time, but I can't exactly complain about that when I'm hardly the model of punctuality myself.

I pulled out my key, the sight of the copy Rachel had made for me brought a smile to my face, and let myself inside.

Two weeks is apparently enough time for habits to form, because I move through the house without thinking now, shutting the door, heading straight for the living room where the old couch I used to crash on has been replaced with a fold-out bed.

The Vicious Hellhound

Late.

Too damn late.

Worrying about something as small as the hour on the clock wasn't how things worked for her, not before few weeks ago, not when the only deadlines that mattered were feeding schedules, fence locks, and when the shop close to her house would close.

Before, being late only mattered if Brian or Lisa called her in for a job, or if she cut it too close getting dogs out of Empire hands before things turned ugly. Those were real concerns, the kind that demanded attention.

This wasn't supposed to be one of them.

Almost two in the morning.

Twenty minutes, in and out, smash-and-grab with her dogs leading the charge while they stripped a jewellery store bare and vanished before anyone could blink.

They'd dodged the PRT cleanly enough, no sirens, no calls coming in hot, but the Empire had been another story. Too close, and only Tattletale's power had kept them from running headlong into a mess they wouldn't have walked away from easily. The capes had been hunting them, clear as day, pissed at them for stealing in their territories, and it only reinforced what Rachel already knew.

And through all of it, every close call and sharp turn and shouted warning, Rachel's thoughts kept circling back to the same thing.

When would this be over so she could go home?

She ground her teeth at the thought. Why did they even need her there for half of that mess? Coordinating this, listening to that, standing around while plans got revised for the fourth time. Just tell her where to go and what to break, and she'd handle the rest.
