"You see something you like, kid?" She said in amusement.

I make sure to stare blankly at her before returning to my drinks, mixing a few things, and going back to analysing the clientele. A side effect of being staff here was the fact that the regulars were getting more acquainted with me.

Next was Mr Hong, a large man in the rotund sense. Whenever he came over, is to order food, never just something to drink. Lose clothes that were obviously of Chinese origin, garbed his body, and followed his calm and precise movements as he ate in peace. We didn't have much, but he always ordered something different than what he got the last time he came.

Spoiler: Mr Hong

Right now, he was just eating silently in the corners of the bar, a plate of carbonara made by yours truly.

The way he comported himself was similar to what I would expect from a monk. Maybe it was the way he carried himself with confidence, but my new Leadership skill seemed to give him a nod of respect.

Also, for some reason, his eyes were always closed.

That's something that I realised that people do in this world. I thought there was just a larger-than-normal amount of blind people, but apparently, they are just capable of seeing even when their eyes were closed.

It was weird, and when I tried to point it out, my acquaintances just shrugged and said that it's always been like this.

I decided not to question any further.

Trip him with a well-placed strike to the knee, keep him grounded there; his weight would work against him if he lost balance.

Bryce Rolando came in last, obviously of some kind of Mexican descent, built like a semi-truck, and just as loud. Even when wearing clothes to cover most of his body, the various tattoos painted on it were easy to identify.

Spoiler: Bryce Rolando

He wore a boisterous grin as he slapped Mr Hong in the shoulder while making his way towards the barstools.

"Still chewing like a cow, man?"

"Still compensating with volume, I see." Was Mr Hong's simple reply.

There was some laughter around the bar, Bryce the loudest amongst them.

He leaned against the counter and gave me the chance to analyse him completely for any type of weapons.

None that I could find. If he was carrying something, it was small enough to hide in his pockets. His stance was sloppy but confident, a confidence definitely acquired from his strong body.

He was dangerous because he believed he was.

Strike the sternum to make him drop his breath, follow it up with a strike to the head to finish it.

"Beer," Bryce said, scoffing at me, "And don't look so thrilled while doing it."

I didn't dignify that with anything, simply sliding the glass over without a word.

He squinted at me, "Man, you ever smile?"

"No." I lied.

"Liar," Kellista snorted, calling me out. Close by, Mr Hong hummed as he finished his plate.

"He is of strong mind and body," Mr Hong said, delicately wiping his mouth with a napkin, "He most reminds me of the sages from back home."

"Or a serial killer," Kelly added helpfully. She also wasn't wrong; you definitely qualified for the title.

Mr Hong smiled, even if it was easy to see that he disagreed.

"What, you think I'm wrong here?" She asked hotly.

Bryce, on the other hand, laughed. "Nah, look at him. Dead eyes and a stiff posture like that? I bet he killed his fair share."

"Oi, since when was my past so interesting?"

"Since we got a bet of two hundred on who gets you to spill." Well then you will remain poor for a long time, cause I ain't saying shit.

"C'mon, can you at least say if we're close?" Kelly drawled out, pulling her current date against her side, who looked remarkably uncomfortable in his position for such a tough-looking man.

"Your guess was hot," I spoke bluntly, watching them all blink. Mr Hong even opened his eyes for a brief second.

I then walk back to the drink, leaving them there, ignoring the sudden deluge of questions that erupted.

Soon, conversation drifted as more regulars chimed in, drinks flowed, and tensions gained by living in this dumpster fire of a city eased. Someone pointed at Bryce's arm.

"Man, don't you have too much ink already? You're not planning on adding more, are you?" I didn't know them, but they were obviously familiar with Bryce.

Bryce scoffed at their words, then grinned, "Tattoos are the story of a man," He said proudly.

"Oh yeah?" Kelly prodded, "Why's there a girl's name crossed out there?"

The bar went quiet for half a second.

"Some stories aren't meant to be told," Bryce said solemnly, then broke into laughter. "Especially not sober."

They laughed with him.

I watched them from behind the counter, hands moving on autopilot as I poured, wiped, mixed.

It didn't take long for the first person to become too inebriated. I noticed it when a guy a few stools down leaned over the counter and couldn't make it more obvious that he was watching me.

"Oi, bartender," the man slurred, leaning too far over the counter, breath heavy with alcohol. "You seem pretty cozy with the girls here. You ever get with any of them?"

Ah.

This poor fucking fool.

He saw that I was black and decided that meant familiarity, or permission, or maybe just courage borrowed from the bottle. Either way, he'd grown far too comfortable.

Around him, the regulars reacted instantly. Chairs shifting to get a better look, those too close drawing away, even as both panic and anticipation slid into their faces.

Yup, they knew exactly what was about to happen and wanted a good seat for it.

"No," I replied evenly, pausing my work long enough to reach beneath the counter and pull out the mop.

If anything, this was convenient. The newer customers had been testing boundaries lately, and reminders were sometimes necessary.

"Shame," he continued, mistaking my calm for encouragement, his grin turning lecherous. "Thought you might've been close with that white girl. Those sluts do anything for a taste."

I didn't look at him as I filled the bucket, water sloshing softly against plastic.

He noticed the silence and, like most idiots, mistook it for an opening.

"C'mon, man," he pressed, lowering his voice as if that made it better. "Tell the truth, she's a slut in bed, isn't she? It's always the excitable ones. How much for me to take her for a spin-"

A large hand landed on his shoulder.

Hard.

The bar went quiet.

Robert stood behind him, broad frame blocking the light, expression carved into a furious glare. The man turned just enough to register who had hold of him, eyes widening a fraction too late.

Hopefully, there won't be too much blood to clean this time.

"You talking about my daughter?" Robert all but growled in restrained violence.

Maybe it was because of the drink in his system, or maybe he just liked his chances against a man who looked like he had a wrestling match with a blender, but the customer didn't back down.

"And if I was?"

The punch came fast.

Teeth scattered across the floor as the man hit the ground on his back, a wet groan tearing out of him while he clutched his face.

"W-wait— I didn't mean— I'm sorry—"

I tuned him out as the demonstration continued.

There were rules here for this neutral zone to work. Don't start shit, don't get into shit, don't bring shit here. That type of stuff.

One of the biggest was simple: don't harass the staff woman. Don't prey on them. Ever. Break that rule, and you're done. Especially when you look to be in your thirties, while the girl you're preying on is sixteen at best.

Robert enforced it personally.

Yeah, pedos got about zero rights in this zone.

Understandable.

By the time the man was dragged outside and thrown onto the street, the bar was still silent, everyone waiting for the sound of the door slamming shut before breathing again.

I moved without comment, mopping the blood from the floor as noise slowly returned, conversation restarting in low murmurs before climbing back to normal levels.

Kelly, who didn't back down from anyone, the leader of the biggest biker gang of Brockton, had lowered her head and watched in amusement, careful to not draw any attention from Robert.

Mr Hong, who watched over the remains of large Asian communities that had been exploited by the ABB, had followed her suit, even if he tried to make it seem like he was focused on his meal.

And Bryce, the man with the largest number of goons, the excess left over from other villains who failed at becoming gang lords, had become quiet in the wake of my boss's anger.

One thing that I learned was that the three of them were the top dogs of gangs around this place. They were leaders to the rabble, enforcers to those who crossed the lines, and the bosses of their own gangs.

But my Boss?

He was the most dangerous one here.

And everyone here knew.

"Who the hell was that?" one of the customers muttered.

Okay, so most of them knew it. The older ones, not the newbies.

And he was obviously new, eyes darting toward the door as if expecting it to explode back open. "I thought this place was neutral ground."

I kept mopping, the rag dragging through diluted blood as I angled myself just enough to listen without looking like I was listening. An important skill of the streets was to look busy, and not draw attention to oneself.

"You don't know?" one of his friends replied, incredulous, turning on his stool to face him. "Man, that was Bull Iron Robert. Owner of Night's Rest, this neutral ground."

"Shit," the newbie breathed, posture straightening. "That a cape?"

"Nah," another chimed in, trying—and failing—to lower his voice. He leaned in anyway, elbows on the table, clearly drunk enough to forget volume control. "He got that name back when he was running a normal gang, years ago."

"That's it?" the first guy scoffed. "Just a gang?"

"Yeah, just that," the drunk one said, nodding with exaggerated emphasis. "Back when Marquis was still around. When the Teeth were active. When the Empire was at its peak. When the fucking Slaughterhouse Nine passed through for a joyride."

A lot of those names flew over my mind, even as the Narrative put its hand up to supply me with the knowledge if I wished. I declined it, because his tone told me enough.

They were enemies, so I would just kill them if the chance came.

"Oh," the newbie said, swallowing. "So he's kind of a big deal."

"You're damn right he is," his friend said. "He pulled out a few years back, when cape fights started getting too brutal for the normal folk. Lost a lot of his people. Once he stepped down, the gang splintered."

"So, he doesn't have a gang right now?"

"He just has this neutral ground. It's honestly a safe haven for people like us," He spoke with a bitter smile, "The gangs don't come near it, nobody really knows why Robert does it, but we don't question a good thing."

The newbie frowned. "And none of the gangs tried to take him out?"

A few of them exchanged looks.

"It's not worth it for them man, like, he's no cape, so his only value is his name,"

"So?" the newbie pressed. "Send some capes after him. Problem solved, right?"

"Well yeah, some tried, but apparently it didn't work."

"What?"

"That's how he manages the neutral zone, because even when the capes came for him, it was the capes that wound up dead in the streets, a message for everyone else."

"Holy shit, this guy is insane. But if he's killing capes, how come the others didn't group up gunning for him?"

"'Cause he ain't a cape man." One of the shrugged, "And he only kills those that come to his territory. If they decide to come kill him off, there's always a chance he can kill more capes in turn. Just stay out of his little pocket, and no one gets hurt."

"Huh," The newbie contemplated.

And thank you for all of that, I muse, dragging the mop into the bucket and wringing it out, water sloshing softly as things clicked into place.

How a neutral zone like this can operate in this feudalistic city of superpowers, why do all the top dogs bow their heads to my boss.

I didn't dwell much on the ramifications of my boss being a killer, mostly because I already thought he was. I also don't really care about the fact that he killed capes, since it seemed like the majority of capes didn't have any innate resistance to getting shot in the face, despite what they liked to believe.

If anything, I wondered why the gang hadn't pushed harder. You would think that anyone who was the leader of a cape gang, and as such had to have ego on themselves, wouldn't allow for any type of competitor for territory, especially a non-parahuman.

Then again, the neutral zone didn't expand. It stayed contained, limited to this district, this bar. Robert did no encroachment, and so he carried forth with no ambition.

As long as Robert didn't threaten their territory, they could afford to let him exist.

And judging by what I'd seen tonight, that arrangement worked just fine for everyone involved.

…Wait.

This arrangement didn't exist because everyone was polite or passive. It existed because nobody crossed the line.

And I had crossed it.

I didn't just beat down ABB members; I did it publicly, then walked straight into Robert's bar and got employed, tying myself to neutral ground like it was nothing. From their perspective, that wasn't just violence, it was an encroachment, a challenge to image and authority, a crack in the unspoken agreement that kept gangs from spilling blood here.

Groups like the ABB didn't let that stand. Reputation was everything to Lung, who used fear as the stick to keep everyone in line. Letting someone like me walk away would make them look weak, and by virtue, make him look weak.

So why is it that after two weeks, nothing has happened yet?

Two weeks. There had been no retaliation. No ambushes as I walked from work. No stalkers in the alleys to follow me home.

Nothing.

I replayed my routine in my head as I pushed the mop across the floor, the bar quieting as the night wound down. The routes I took home. The corners I cut. The way I deliberately left myself exposed, almost daring someone to try something.

And then it clicked.

They hadn't stayed away from me.

They'd stayed away from here.

From Robert's territory.

…Was Robert protecting me?​

[FEAT: Figure out the piece that was missing from the puzzle.
1x Random Bronze Gacha Ticket]
​
I blinked, breath catching as a wash of unfamiliar emotion rolled through me, sharp and messy and hard to name. Anger came first, hot and immediate, followed by something worse beneath it.

That stupid, fucking idiot.

I let go of the mop, water dripping back into the bucket as I turned, already moving toward the back, intent on confronting him, on demanding answers—

The front door slammed open.

The sound cracked through the bar like a gunshot.

I turned slowly, already knowing what I'd see; everyone else was tense and watching the entrance.

Of course. Of course, now.

They poured in with practiced confidence, ten of them at least, spreading out just enough to block exits without making it obvious. Red and yellow marked their jackets and bandanas, dragon motifs curling across fabric and skin alike.

The one who stepped forward was tall and broad, muscles packed onto his frame like it had been carved there, fire and dragon tattoos crawling up his arms and disappearing beneath his collar. His smile was easy, relaxed, and didn't bother hiding what it promised.

Violence.

"Evenin'," he said with an obvious accent, voice smooth as oil over water. "We were hopin' Flatline might be interested in a little conversation."

I straightened, planting my feet behind the bar, shoulders relaxed, hands loose at my sides. I felt no fear, my mind immediately jumping into a simulation of how to be efficient as I took them all down. Each carried a weapon, some in the form of bats, while others carried a few pieces by the back, there to look menacing. The one who talked had nothing on him.

Space was tight, I could stay close to the front line, and they would have to risk shooting their own man. No, I can't let the customers be hurt either.

"Unless you're ordering a drink," I replied flatly, "you can leave."

A few of them chuckled. The leader's smile widened.

"Ah, shame," he said, shaking his head as if genuinely disappointed. "See, it's just that we ran into a couple of lost girls tonight. Real confused. Real scared."

My thoughts slammed to a halt.

The bar seemed to recede, sound dulling as my attention narrowed.

"And we figured," he continued, voice lowering just enough to crawl under the skin, "that maybe you'd wanna hear about it."

Something stirred behind my throat.

"Now, you may address me as Hiroshi," he finished, tilting his head, eyes glittering with satisfaction, "are you willing to talk?"

I couldn't help it, I laughed.

That seemed to shock them, the leader especially, completely frozen in place as if he couldn't begin to understand what I was laughing at.

"I see," He tried to speak, to keep the control he had going, "So you do not care what ha-"

"Call them." I break him off, my laughter subsiding as I stare at him blankly.

Brows furrowing, Hiroshi seemed irritated at my cutting of his words.

"Oh?"

"You fucked up, and you don't even know how," I said in amusement, before opening a palm to him, "Call them."

The situation was tense, but Leadership wasn't just about commanding your men; it was about understanding and making others follow your words.

Under my order, the man begrudgingly took a phone and dialled a number.

No one picked up.

I smiled.

He held my stare for a bit, but I could see the cracks forming, the sweat beginning to fall from the pressure. Once again, he dialled the number, and we waited in the silence as it rang.

The man looked at his phone with dawning terror, realisation finally flooding in as he understood what happened.

There was no one on the other side to pick up the phone.

"What did you do?" Hiroshi said, horror dripping through his words.

But I don't answer him, throwing my apron across the counter, I make myself comfortable in one of the tables of the bar.

The thing is, I didn't do anything.

And the question he should be asking was 'Who did we fuck with?'

But about two weeks ago, when I wasn't aware of how the Gacha worked, I didn't question an event.

When I beat down the ABB members who surrounded Pritt and Leah, I did not receive any rewards for my action of saving them.

I never thought deeply into that, but as I worked with both of them throughout the two weeks, the answer became obvious.

They were never in any danger.

"Sit down." I point at the now freed chair before me, "You wanted to talk, didn't you?"

After all, just like I realised all those days ago.

Pritt Martinez was the most dangerous individual in this bar.​

Spoiler: On the other side of the Phone
"Let's talk."

#382
POV: Samuel Rodrigues

The bar went quiet after my words settled in the air, not that I didn't anything else to follow. A few hands tightened around glasses or drifted a little closer to jackets and belts, closer to where their guns were held. Thankfully no one panicked, even if the newbies looked like they were shitting bricks.

Heh, a few people were even looking at me in awe, which was very misplaced.

It made sense, I had just openly claimed responsibility for foiling his plan I wasn't even physically present for, and even if I knew the details, none of the people here did.

I made a mental note to check on Pritt and Leah after this was over. If the ABB was bold enough to walk in here, then the ripples from what happened earlier weren't done spreading yet. Pritt being a Parahuman meant complications, which meant I would probably have to deal with he fallout of this shit.

Those were problems for later.

Right now, all of my focus was on the men in front of me.

The one at the center of the group took a few seconds too long to speak, his eyes flicking briefly to his subordinates before returning to me. Hiroshi was shaken. He hid it well, holding himself together through sheer force of will, not a crack on his posture that would say anything about how a piece of his operation went wrong.

The dim lighting of the bar worked in his favor, shadows cutting across his face and softening the strain around his eyes. Whatever uncertainty Hiroshi felt, he buried it quickly as he met my gaze, pulling it down behind a mask that looked practiced.

After a few more tense moments, he stepped forward and took the seat across from me, placing himself deliberately within arm's reach. His back straightened, chin lifting upwards, looking down at me.

"You think you'll get out of this alive?" Hiroshi asked.

Ah, I see we'll start things off with threats now, won't we?

I leaned my cheek into my knuckles, elbow resting against the table, my posture loose in contrast to his stiffness. Sitting with my back to the wall had been intentional. From here, any weapon drawn would be pointed at me first, which meant I didn't have to worry about dodging, as no one would be behind me.

"I think my chances are better than yours," I replied evenly.

A flicker passed through his eyes at that, irritation sharpened by the low murmurs behind him. One of the men standing a step back shifted his weight, teeth grinding audibly as my dismissal sank in, obviously not very disciplined by the way his hands clenched around his submachine gun.

Side note, this feels nostalgic.

Hiroshi's lips pressed into a thin line. "Do not mistake your momentary display for power," he said, voice calm but coiled. "You are nothing but a pebble on the roadside. Easily crushed and just as easily forgotten."

"This pebble sure messed up a few of your boys." I said, tilting my head slightly. "How are they doing, by the way? Still eating through straws?"

They should be. I made sure to give them quite a few fractures.

Hiroshi's composure cracked for just a fraction of a second, the flash of anger unable to contain itself, his jaw tightening before he smoothed his expression again. He smiled then, eyes narrowing as they curved shut in a way that didn't reach his face, reminding me too much of a fucking snake.

"Yes," he said softly. "And that is precisely why I am here."

He leaned forward just enough to lower his voice, though he didn't bother trying to keep it truly private. "Your actions have caused unrest among our younger members. Dissent quickly rose as you walked off scot-free, it made people think us weak."

"You aren't?" I tilt my head, and it causes Hiroshi's face to briefly spazz with incredibility, followed by anger.

Hehehe, its surprisingly funny to bait the rage of others, maybe I should listen to more of those stupid videos that Pritt watches?

"There were attempts to rectify their views. But the leader of this neutral zone," He says roughly, almost spitting the word out, "Made it clear that he cares not for our authority."

So that's what Robert was protecting me from, the idiot. How come I never heard of it? Unless Robert employed other people to 'protect' me? Ah fuck, did he know that Pritt was a Parahuman- were they in cahoots here?

[FEAT: Figure out another piece that was missing from the puzzle.
1x Random Bronze Gacha Ticket]
​
"But worry not," The smile he gave me was anything but real, "We made sure to keep him occupied."

I had been wondering when Robert would walk back in, but I guess there is my answer.

Man, these guys just don't learn, do they?

"In the end however, we cannot look away from this insult. You can no longer hide behind the so-called neutral zone." There was obvious disdain at the mere idea of a 'neutral zone', and it made me think.

"You will come with us," Hiroshi continued, confidence settling back into his posture like armor. "You will be presented before the Dragon, and your public execution will serve as an example. A reminder of what happens to those who stain our reputation."

That seemed… excessive, especially since I thought that Robert would be the one who would get executed for openly defying the ABB. But then again, they probably believe that it would be easier to get a kid then Bull Iron Robert.

I paused, considering it. Having their orders, their wishes being pushed back outside of their territory wouldn't be enough for this level of response.

In a normal gang, it wouldn't. But this wasn't normal. Parahumans warped everything. Reputation wasn't just about respect in this world, it was survival, and allowing a loss like that to stand uncontested would invite challengers, each of who could be a cape with a specific power that might just be catastrophic.

Still.

This felt like more than retaliation.

They were pushing too hard, too publicly, for something this small.

Which meant there was more to it.

"That's not much of an incentive to come with you," I pointed out, tone flat as I leaned back slightly in my chair.

Threatening someone into cooperation while openly telling them they were going to die for it felt backwards. Then again, this was a world where villains made speeches and heroes did Live in events for posteriority, I guess you're allowed to be more dramatic.

Hiroshi's eyes hardened.

"Must I remind you that you do not have a choice, Flatline?" he said.

The shift was immediate. The soft clink that I was used to, of safety's being switch off rang out, and suddenly every gun in the ABB's possession was pointed directly at me.

Ugh, he's using that annoying nickname. Did they even bother learning my real name?

"And with your execution," Hiroshi continued, voice growing louder, almost theatrical, "there will be an announcement."

He stood and turned, arms spreading slightly as if addressing an audience which the bar definitely didn't agree to be one. The bar was packed, and nobody moved. People stayed rigidly stayed seated, because standing up meant drawing attention, and attention right now came with a gun barrel attached.

"The neutral zone," he said, savouring the words, "will be officially annexed. From this moment on, it will fall under ABB jurisdiction."

Ah, there it is.

It was a shelter. A pocket of calm carved out of a city that didn't allow softness. A place where people could breathe without constantly watching their backs, where fear loosened its grip just enough to remind these people what normal life felt like.

It was protection.

It was the literal heart of Downtown, the east center of the city and where people can-

Ah, I get it now.

"I'm just an excuse," I said, cutting through the noise.

Hiroshi glanced back at me, clearly amused now that he felt like he had the upper hand in the dynamics here, "Oh?"

"You don't care about some territory dispute," I continued. "The ABB were planning to take this place anyway. You all just needed a reason that didn't make you look desperate."

Night's Rest was in a pretty good strategical position after all, close enough to the Docks, which is ABB territory, while also bordering on the side of reasonably good ending businesses.

If they get this district, they can come in and swoop it all under ABB banner.

His smile widened, it gave off an oily feeling, clearly pleased I'd caught on, and not giving a shit about being called off, because in the end it didn't matter.

"With this district under our purview," Hiroshi said calmly, "all who refuse to submit to the ABB will be crushed."

The way his gaze slid toward Kelly and Bryce wasn't subtle. It lingered, his eyes and smile mocking them deliberately. Neither of them looked away. Kelly's grin stayed sharp as she bared her teeth, while Bryce's jaw clenched and he glared at Hiroshi.

When Hiroshi looked at Mr. Hong, though, there was a pause.

The older man met his stare with a soft, almost gentle smile, hands folded neatly in front of him as if this was a polite conversation instead of a threat display.

Still, how annoying, am I being dismissed?

"Oi," I snapped, flicking my fingers sharply like I'm calling for a dog. "Eyes on me, bastard."

The sound cut clean through the room.

Hiroshi turned on reflex, anger already breaking across his face, mouth opening with a snarl—

And then he stopped.

Because when he met my eyes, I allowed the Oni to shine.

There was no need to transform. Calling upon that pressure without a horn, or my skin turning ashy red was effortless, a weight that came from me being something closer to a natural disaster than a person.

It bled into the space between us, an oppressive miasma that dragged upon the air itself.

My displeasure made manifest.

Spoiler: What Hiroshi sees

"You don't seem to understand," I said, leaning forward, my voice low and even as I stared him down. "So let me make this simple."

This man wasn't a threat. He was an irritation. A pest that had wandered into the wrong place and ruined what had been a decent night.

"All who drink here," I continued, "are under my protection."

There was no need to flourish my words, they were plain, because they were true.

"And if you became a threat," The words also came out naturally, a weird feeling, because I myself felt surprised by how easy they flowed out of my mouth, "You become trash for me to throw out."

Keeping the peace here had been my job. Making sure things didn't spiral. Making sure people could come in, relax, laugh, and leave without something following them home.

Somewhere along the way, I'd stopped thinking of it as just work.

I'd grown used to it.

I'd grown to like it.

The peace, and the people who I kept it for.

And as the weight of my declaration settled, I felt it—Leadership flaring naturally—as the crowd straightened behind them.

Hiroshi stared at me, eyes unsteady, his composure visibly straining under the weight of my words. For a moment, I thought he might actually falter. Then, with what looked like genuine effort, he forced out a laugh.

It was the kind of laugh an adult gives a child who just declared they were going to grow wings and fly. Loud enough to reassure himself.

"How arrogant," he said, shaking his head slowly. "Can you truly protect everyone here, Flatline? And even if you could, how do you plan to stop those who will come after? How do you plan to stop the dragon that will rage when you do?"

"I'll deal with Lung."

I said it plainly, stating the obvious.

The ABB weren't complicated. They were a gang held together by fear and momentum, and the Narrative helpfully filled in the gaps as the thought formed. Lung ruled through sheer power and fear, his presence violent enough that rival Asian ethnicities, groups that would normally tear each other apart, stayed in line under him. He was the spine of the ABB. The threat that kept everything from collapsing.

If I killed Lung, the ABB would fracture. It wouldn't be clean. There would be infighting, power grabs, plenty of blood spilled as smaller groups scrambled to take his place.

But they wouldn't be able to touch this district anymore.

And if chaos followed?

I'd deal with that too.

The bar went dead silent.

Hiroshi's expression shifted, disbelief creeping in before he threw his head back and laughed again. This time it was genuine. This time his tone was truly ridiculing, his mind placing me in the same category as the dirt on the ground.

"You'll deal with Lung?" he said between chuckles, looking at me like I'd just told a bad joke. "I thought you were slow before, but perhaps you are truly ill of mind. Child, Lung is powerful—far beyond anything you can comprehend. Do not insult him with fantasies."

How pointless.

So, what if Lung was stronger?

I won't lose.

"It doesn't matter if he is or not," I said, my face remaining flat as the words came, Leadership guiding them, hinting at how to truly speak and be heard, "These people came into our bar. They drank from our cups."

I could feel it as I spoke, something subtle shifting in the room.

"They trusted me," I continued. "As long as I'm breathing, nothing happens to them."

The crowd stirred again, a low movement rippling through the bar. Some stared at me like I'd lost my mind, eyes wide. But what impresses me is that my words seemed to move just as many, their faces setting into hard stony visages, hands trailing for their guns.

Kelly grinned harder, hands already neatly resting on the guns by her side.

Bryce was rigid, ready to spring forward at any moment.

All of their followers, noticing their leaders, also got set.

That mattered more than Hiroshi's opinion ever could.

I had decided to protect them. A decision that was made by me and me alone, one made in an act to live a life unlike my last one.

A decision I have yet to regret.

A decision that would not change until my heart stopped.

My words finally cracked the mask, anger at my confidence rising. With a sharp movement, he drew the pistol I noted he carried by his side, the metal glinting under the bar lights as he levelled it at my forehead. I could dodge it easily. I could disarm him just as fast.

Most held their breath at the drawing of a firearm, but Hiroshi ignored it.

I didn't move.

Attention had to be on me, I needed to have all eyes on me for this.

"Well?" he snarled, the tremor in his voice bleeding through despite his effort to hide it. "Where's that strength now? Where's your promise? Will you keep it when I blow your fucking head off?"

Good. All eyes on me.

"Try me."

I leaned forward, closing the distance myself, pressing my forehead against the cold barrel of the gun. I didn't break eye contact.

His grip shook, he obviously shot others before, you didn't rise through the ranks of a gang lead by someone as ruthless as Lung without having blood on your hands.

But how many times did you look someone in the eyes when you pulled the trigger?

Well, Hiroshi, I'm betting that not many.

My movement was enough to set everything off.

The moment I leaned into the gun, every ABB member reacted, weapons snapping up as their instincts screamed at them to follow their leader's example. Their attention locked onto me in a tight, panicked focus, fingers tense on triggers, eyes darting between my face and Hiroshi's trembling hand.

And in doing so, they missed it.

They didn't see the way the crowd shifted slowly, it kind of reminded me of that one time I went to the beach, a tide pulling back before a wave. Moving silently, not letting the chairs scrap against the floor, and boots are repositioned, with bodies angled just enough to give clear lines of fire.

The ABB members are unaware of what happens around them, their focus on me as veterans moved, the years of living in Brockton Bay teaching them exactly when it was time to stop pretending to be civilians.

Watching it happen sent a strange warmth through my chest.

Leadership really was terrifying. I hadn't said a word to coordinate this, hadn't given a signal or a plan, yet everyone understood what was being asked of them. They trusted me to stand, so they stood with me.

"Listen to my words," I said calmly, the corner of my mouth lifting despite myself.

Hiroshi flinched at the sight of my smile, his bravado cracking just enough for him to realize he'd misjudged something important.

"Don't threaten the guy who pours the drink."

The bar exploded into motion.

Guns came up with the speed that only came with experience, their movements exactly the same as I remember the top enforcers of my old gang doing, veterans of an unstable city. In the span of a heartbeat, every tattooed man found themselves staring down barrels instead of levelling them.

They were surrounded.

"Don't move a muscle, shithead."

Kelly was already there, the flick of her hand fast enough that normal me would be hard pressed to react, one of her Berettas pressed firmly against the back of a gunman's head. I had a distinct feeling that this was a lot more personal for her given the absolute ecstasy filling her smile right now.

"Hahaha! You weren't lying—Flatline can smile!"

Bryce's laughter rang out with adrenaline. He didn't have a gun, but he didn't need one, the knife in his hand hovering a breath away from another ABB member's throat, close enough that even swallowing looked dangerous.

Dammit.

I hadn't even realized I was smiling.

The thought should have embarrassed me. Instead, it only made the smile widen.

Mr. Hong stood a little apart from the chaos, his expression as serene as ever, as though this were nothing more than an inconvenience to be corrected. The shotgun in his hands, however, told a very different story.

"I believe," he said mildly, "that you have all been rude enough."

Scary man he was, I didn't even notice where he'd hidden a weapon that large.

Shouts rippled through the ABB group as they realized just how badly the situation had flipped, eyes wide as they took in the wall of guns pointed at them, yet none dared make any risky moves. Any sudden movement now would end with their blood on the floor, and they knew it.

Hiroshi looked around in disbelief, his face pale as the truth finally settled in.

That distraction was all I needed.

I slapped the pistol from his hand with an easy motion, sending it skittering across the floor before he could even react. His mouth opened to shout "You—!"

—and then cut off sharply as the butt of my Glock met his face.

The weapon appeared in my hand as I moved, the motion smooth enough that it looked like I'd drawn it from somewhere behind my back. Hiroshi hit the floor hard, clutching his face as he groaned in pain.

I stood, stepped forward, and planted my foot squarely on his chest, pinning him to the ground as I levelled the gun at his head.

From this position, he looked small.

He looks up at me in fear. The anguish, the anger, everything else was there, but they were overshadowed by the primal terror that came from having a gun pointed your way, stripping a man down to exactly what he was when death was only a twitch away.

A sight so pathetic my smile fades.

"W-wait!" he gasped, hands coming up in surrender. "Do you not care about your boss?!"

I paused.

I had been wondering where Robert was. He'd stepped out earlier to deal with a that troublesome customer and hadn't returned yet, which had been nagging at the back of my mind.

Hiroshi seized on the hesitation like a drowning man grabbing at driftwood.

"Y-yeah! I don't know what you did to protect those girls, but if you don't listen to me, that old man is gonna fucking get it—"

The door exploded inward.

We all turned as a body just in time to see an ABB member fly through the entrance, crashing onto the floor in a heap of limbs and blood. Teeth scattered across the wood, and even without looking closely, it was obvious he'd taken more than a few concussions.

A second later, Robert stepped inside.

His scarred face was carved into a thunderous scowl as he dragged another unconscious ABB member behind him by the collar, tossing the man aside like trash once he crossed the threshold.

"The fuck are you all doing in my bar?" he growled.

I scanned him quickly, relief washing over me when I saw nothing worse than a few scratches. Nothing life-threatening.

"Don't worry, boss," I said, straightening as I holstered my gun. "I'll just be showing them out."

Robert watched me for a moment, eyes narrowed, the tension in his body slowly easing now that the immediate threat was gone. Then his expression twisted into irritation as his gaze dropped to the floor.

"Hmph," he muttered, before scowling. "Then why the hell is there still blood on my floor?"

Ah. Shit.

"That's their fault," I said shamelessly, pointing at the ABB.

The effect was immediate. Every single one of them flinched as Robert's glare snapped their way, the promise of violence etched on it if they gave him even the slightest excuse. Around the bar, a few customers let out short, disbelieving laughs.

I watched Hiroshi in particular.

Whatever hope he'd been clinging to had finally drained out of him, his posture sagging as the full scale of how badly this had gone sank in. The fear in his eyes wasn't just about the guns anymore, but about the realization that nothing he'd planned tonight had survived first contact.

"All of you," I said, my voice cutting cleanly through the noise, "drop your weapons."

My gaze moved over them one by one, lingering just long enough to make the threat feel personal. They were shaking now, hands unsteady, breaths shallow as they weighed their chances and found nothing but bad outcomes waiting for them.

I could feel it working.

My stance, my expression, the way my voice carried through the room, it all came together as I leaned fully into Leadership, layering it with the pressure of the Oni until I felt like the center of gravity in the bar. Right now, I wasn't just a bartender or some kid who'd gotten lucky, I was the authority, and they knew it.

For a long moment, nobody moved.

Then one of them cracked.

His gun hit the floor with a dull clatter, the man who you think couldn't even be twenty was shaking. That was all it took, soon the rest followed in a rush, weapons dropping one after the other as the dam broke, fear winning out over loyalty.

"No, no!" Hiroshi gasped, his voice climbing as panic set in. "Lung won't forget this! You hear me? This cowardice will be the fucking end of you—"

My foot drove into his stomach before he could finish the thought.

The air left him in a wheezing cry as he doubled over, collapsing to the floor and clutching his gut. He wasn't saying anything else for a while.

"You," I said, pointing toward one of the customers, a broad-shouldered guy who'd been watching all of this with sharp, focused eyes. "Collect the guns. Put them behind the counter."

He didn't hesitate, and I marvelled at how Leadership made this so much easier then it had any right to be.

In seconds, he was moving through the pile of weapons, scooping them up efficiently while the ABB stood there, empty-handed and very aware of it. By the time he finished, they looked stripped down to what they really were, a group of scared men far from home.

I waited until I had all of their attention again, my gaze flat and unblinking as it swept across them.

"This is your only warning," I said, slowly curling my hand into a fist, the quiet sound of bone shifting under pressure carrying in the sudden hush. "Go back to your dens, little snakes, and don't look back."

I needed them alive.

Not out of mercy, but because fear travelled better when there were witnesses to spread it. The more of them that walked out of here breathing, the harder it would be for anyone to twist what had happened tonight. Their terror would do the work for me, a tactic I knew all to well, a tactic I have played before.

Every non-cape gang in this district would hear about this. Every neutral party would remember who stood when the ABB tried to take their safe ground.

A united front mattered.

I nodded, and my allies slowly lowered their weapons, giving the ABB just enough space to move. Without ceremony, I picked up Hiroshi, and threw him toward his men, sending him rolling across the floor with a pained cry until someone hauled him upright.

Beneath the weight of my eyes, these man who spread misery did not posture any further.

They fled.

Boots pounded against the floor as they rushed for the exit, curses spilling out between laboured breaths, none of them daring to look back as they vanished into the night.

The door slammed shut.

For a moment, the bar was silent.

Then—

"Holy shit," Bryce said, breaking into a grin as he looked around. "Did Flatline just start a gang war?"

I rubbed my face, letting out a slow breath as the adrenaline finally began to ebb.

Yes, Bryce.

Yes, I absolutely fucking did.

[FEAT: Deescalate an armed conflict!

1x Random Silver Advantage Gacha Ticket]


[FEAT: Start a gang war with the ABB!

1x Random Gold Gacha Ticket]

​
Well, at least it can't get more complicated than this.


Ten Minutes Later…

I stared at the four people sitting across from me with the dead, hollow look.

Bryce, Kelly, Mr. Hong, Robert and I were all seated around the table, the problem here is that they all wore various matching grins that seemed to be at my expense.

Then, as if rehearsed, all four of them raised their glasses.

"A toast to our newest bossman!" Bryce announced

"Banzai!" Mr Hong cheered along.

"Yooho!" Kelly rallied alongside him.

"Hmm," Robert simply hummed approvingly.

As they chugged down their drinks, I could only blink down at my own cup, filled with Sake.

Bob.

I know this is somehow your fucking fault.

[FEAT: Became the Villainous Leader of a Gang!

1x Random Platinum Gacha Ticket]

​

#452
POV: Samuel Rodrigues

So it turns out there was a second floor to the bar, which I had absolutely no idea about. Too bad, really, since securing a building became a hell of a lot easier when you had natural choke points like a staircase.

After the little debacle with the ABB, Robert called a meeting with the other local bosses.

I wasn't entirely sure why the hell I was included. And considering I had spent the entire evening around other people and still hadn't had the chance to send my Glock back into its item space through the Gacha. The weight of it was a constant reminder at my side as we climbed the stairs, hopefully no one would question it.

We all ended up seated around a single table, the room quiet as we waited for Robert to begin, since he was the one who had called this meeting.

Only instead of talking, the man moved behind the bar up here and started serving drinks, methodically pouring each of us a glass with unhurried care. I recognized the tactic since my own old bosses used it a lot, a type of slow silence that people used to apply pressure and make others fill the space for them. One by one, the glasses were placed down in front of everyone.

Everyone except me.

"You're fifteen," Robert said flatly, catching the look on my face before I could even complain.

My Oni self-bristled at the injustice. It demanded alcohol on principle.

Is it really alcoholism if your inner demon runs on the stuff?

"This guy is fifteen?" Bryce blinked, looking me up and down, then glancing down at his own arms as if comparing muscle mass.

I just shrugged.

"Damn," Kelly muttered, "you do not look fifteen."

I took that as a compliment and nodded, letting the silence settle again as Robert finally took his own drink and leaned against the counter.

Unfortunately, he still didn't say anything, just sipped and ignored the collective stares burning into him.

"Sooo," Bryce eventually said, setting his glass down with a soft clink, "what do we do?"

"The fuck is this 'we'?" Kelly shot back immediately, leaning into her chair. "You speaking French or something?"

"Drop the tough bitch act," Bryce replied without missing a beat, rolling his eyes. "We both know none of us survives the ABB on our own."

Kelly clicked her tongue at that, clearly irritated, but she didn't actually argue the point.

"The fuckers expect us to roll over the moment they roll out the red carpet," she said instead, anger bleeding through her words. There was something personal in it, that kind of resentment didn't come from tonight alone.

"We wouldn't have any other option," Robert cut in, his voice calm but firm.

Both Bryce and Kelly bristled at his tone, shifting in their seats, but neither of them actually refuted him.

It made sense. I wasn't completely sure of all the details yet, but after a few weeks in this world, I'd already noticed the divide between Parahumans and normal people. Digging around online using Pritt's phone, I'd learned that a lot of people genuinely considered Parahumans untouchable by normies, which was honestly hilariously stupid.

Most of them didn't even have any kind of constitution enhancement. A gun to the head worked just as well on them as it did on anyone else, and yet they still acted as the central pillars of both government institutions and gang hierarchies.

Power did weird things to people's perception.

"And what shall we do now, then?" Mr. Hong asked, folding his hands on the table.

Bryce exhaled heavily, rubbing the back of his neck. "I need to gather my boys. We've got enough people to move our assets, but we'll probably have to relocate to another city."

"If you need supplies moved," Kelly said, smirking as she rubbed her fingers together in a clear money gesture, "my streets are open. Just give the right incentive."

I see.

They weren't planning to stay and fight, which made sense. From what I'd gathered, Bryce was the only one with enough people to even consider mounting any real resistance. Mr. Hong had numbers, but most of them weren't criminals, just people trying to survive in refugee areas. Kelly had the smallest group, but nearly all of hers had vehicles and knew how to use them.

Individually, they'd be slaughtered by the ABB.

Still…

"You're all making this too complicated," I said, leaning back in my chair and crossing my arms as I looked around the table.

"Oh?" Kelly tilted her head toward me, one brow lifting as her expression hardened. "And what the hell do you mean by that?"

"I said I would protect you all," I replied evenly. "And I will."

"Kid," Bryce said, shaking his head slightly as he leaned forward, "not that we don't appreciate the sentiment, but this is an entire gang we're talking about."

"And don't even start with their capes," Kelly cut in, waving the idea away with a sharp flick of her wrist, the outcome obviously already decided. "Without one of our own, we don't compete. That's just reality."

I understood where they were coming from. Lung was the kind of Parahuman I wouldn't have been able to touch in my old body, not unless I was holding an anti-tank rifle from a kilometer away and strapped head to toe in explosives.

Exponential growth in strength was a ridiculous ability, one that warped any fight into something unfair by default.

Still, none of that actually mattered, since I could just kill Lung.

I had already told them I would protect them, but words alone weren't enough. Without their acknowledgment, without their belief, my job would be infinitely harder.

So how do I make them trust my word?

Leadership, I'll trust your instincts.

"What is Night's Rest?" I asked calmly, the sharper words I wanted to use held back as I let them reshape into something that would actually reach them.

"It's a safe zone, an area where we don't fight," I continued, lifting my hands slightly as I spoke and letting my gaze settle on each of them in turn. "A place where men don't worry about being attacked, and women don't fear disappearing into the night."

They were listening now

"You are all part of this zone," I said, emphasizing the word as I leaned forward. "You all came here because you believed in us, because you trusted that these rules of peace would be upheld."

In the weeks I'd worked here, I'd watched people smile, watched laughter come easier when fear wasn't hovering over every conversation, and somewhere along the way I'd realized that this, right here, was what I actually wanted to do.

I wanted to help this zone that would have definitely helped me when I was a child in a dangerous world.

"I've seen what safety does for people," I continued, my fingers curling into a fist as I felt something stir beneath my skin, the weight of being a Calamity leaking into my voice whether I meant it to or not. "And I know this is worth protecting."

I looked at each of them, holding their gazes even as discomfort crept in.

"Answer me this," I asked. "Do you really want to abandon what's yours? Are you willing to just let go of this ideal of peace?"

Only Robert met my stare without flinching.

The rest hesitated, and I knew why. Even as they talked about leaving the city, I could hear the anger beneath their words, feel the grief and frustration that came with being forced to bow to the overwhelming strength that was the ABB.

"And what are we supposed to do?" Kelly snapped, her teeth clenched as she pushed back in her chair. "Fight here and die for a fucking ideal?"

"Be reasonable, kid," Bryce added in a low murmur as he rubbed his temple, "There's no version of this where we come out on top."

God, they still didn't get it.

"You're still members of Night's Rest," I said, and this time I stopped holding back the Calamity that wished to come to the surface.

The transformation rolled through me slowly. My frame expanded as muscle pressed against fabric, the chair beneath me creaking faintly as my weight shifted. My skin darkened into an ashy red, heat radiating outward as a single horn pushed through my forehead.

The room fell silent.

"Oh my," Mr. Hong murmured, the only one who seemed capable of speech as the others stared with wide eyes.

"Ah," Robert huffed, nodding to himself as if something had finally clicked into place. "So you were a Parahuman."

"'Ah' my fucking ass," Kelly snapped, half rising from her chair, agitation flashing across her face. "You're a Parahuman, Flatline? Why the hell would you just show us something like that?"

"Because I will protect you," I said, my voice deeper now, resonant enough that it seemed to settle into the walls themselves.

There was a heavy silence after my words, thick enough to pull everyone out of their spiralling thoughts and leave them simply staring at me.

"If capes are what we need to level the playing field," I continued, my voice steady as I let the moment stretch just enough, "then that's what we'll do."

In this world, Parahumans made up the strongest concentration of power in any organization worth a damn. Gangs rose and fell around them, built their reputations on them, and feared them. If I were one, then that alone would be enough to become a stabilizing pillar for the smaller groups who were already planning to leave.

"Fuck, you're—" Bryce started, then broke off into a disbelieving laugh as he dragged a hand down his face. "Even if you're a cape, how exactly are you planning to fight the entire ABB just to protect a safe zone?"

"I'll need help," I answered without hesitation.

At the end of the day, I was still one person. Until the Gacha decided to hand me the ability to exist in two places at once, I could only be where I was, and that limitation mattered more than raw power at the moment.

Their help, specifically, would smooth things out a lot. These weren't nobodies. They were the leaders of the main non-Parahuman gangs in the area, and if they stood together against the ABB, it would only be a matter of time before the smaller groups were folded into them, whether out of necessity or survival instinct.

"You want our help?" Mr. Hong asked, his round face tilting slightly as he studied me.

"Yes."

"And what's in it for us?" Bryce asked, blunt as ever.

I didn't begrudge him the question. They lived as criminals, after all, and doing things for free was a quick way to end up used, discarded, or buried.

Well, if you were weak enough for that to happen.

"Expansion of the neutral zone," I said. This time, I followed the path Leadership laid out for me, letting the idea unfold naturally instead of forcing it. "I want your help taking ABB territory and turning it into ours."

The silence that followed wasn't empty, this time as they all thought over my words.

"Ours?" Kelly repeated, her eyes narrowing slightly.

I nodded. "The neutral zone is a place of protection first and foremost, isn't it?"

"I said it before, and I'll say it again," I continued, lifting my hand slightly as the glass looked almost too small in my partially transformed grip. "I will protect everyone who falls under our jurisdiction."

The weight of my presence lingered following my words.

"And since I started working here," I said, letting my gaze move across them, "I saw you all for what you really are. People who come here to laugh, to drink, to breathe without fear of a bullet to the back."

I finished the last sip of water and set the glass down before lifting my eyes again, focused now, intent enough to pin each of them in place.

"So, here's the question," I said quietly. "Will you fight for that? For the chance to smile again as you did before tonight?"

They sat with it. I had just asked them to unite and stand against one of the largest, most entrenched gangs in Brockton Bay.

But my words didn't waver, because they were the truth, because the people before me were under my protection, and I needed help to protect others.

"Yes," Robert said at last. "I will."

Yeah, I thought you would be the first one to agree, Boss.

He met my gaze, and the fire there mirrored my own. This was a man who had survived in a city full of monsters without ever becoming one himself, a normal human who had learned how to stand his ground among giants.

"Ah, what the hell?" Bryce said, laughing as he slammed his drink onto the table. "I like this place—no, scratch that, I love this place. Where else am I gonna drink without a gun pointed at my head?"

"Ugh," Kelly muttered, shaking her head as if annoyed with herself. "You bastards would have way too much fun without me." Then she grinned, fingers idly brushing against her holsters. "Count me in."

"Hohoho," Mr. Hong chuckled softly, eyes gleaming. "It seems everyone is rather fired up. Very well, then. I shall throw my hat into the ring as well."

Good. This was good.

Leadership really was a terrifyingly useful skill. Rallying people by identifying what they already wanted, what they were afraid to lose, felt almost effortless once I stopped fighting the instinct.

None of them wanted to leave now. All three knew exactly what was at stake, knew that staying meant risking everything they had built.

But none of them wanted to lose this place.

None of them truly wanted to give up this small, fragile slice of heaven they'd carved out in the middle of this shithole of a city that seemed determined to crush it.

The threat the ABB posed was dire, not because of numbers or territory, but because of the strength Lung represented. If I wanted others to rally behind my commands, then words alone wouldn't be enough. I needed to show them something greater, something undeniable.

"Well," Kelly said, leaning back slightly in her chair as she eyed me, "we've got one cape on our side already, which is a pretty big deal. If we're really committing to this, we can't just send you off to handle every little thing—"

"My power isn't limited to this," I cut in before she could steer the conversation further down that path.

"Oh?" Mr. Hong asked, curiosity sharpening his tone as he looked between us.

"This transformation," I said, gesturing faintly to myself, "is that of an Oni."

Understanding flickered across Mr. Hong's face almost immediately.

"I see," he said, nodding slowly. "Not only a physical transformation, but accompanying abilities as well?"

"Oni?" Bryce asked, blinking. "You're gonna have to help me out here."

"In Japanese folklore," Mr. Hong explained smoothly, "Oni are mythological beings of immense spiritual power, often associated with strength, calamity, and excess. Creatures said to live on sake and meat."

That tracked a little too well, honestly. I wasn't surprised Mr. Hong knew this; he struck me as the kind of man who studied mythology as a way to better understand the cultures he dealt with.

"You're correct," I said. "My power reflects the mythological aspect more than anything else."

I lifted both hands. A small flame bloomed at the tip of my fingers on one side, wavering gently in the air, while with the other, I twisted my wrist and pulled a glob of water from the bottle at the side, letting it float lazily above my palm.

Explaining the entire Gacha system would have been a nightmare, not to mention completely unbelievable, so Leadership nudged me toward easing them into the idea instead. I didn't enjoy lying to them like this, but telling the outright truth would be basically game over on this front.

"Damn," Bryce muttered, eyes never leaving my hands. "You really won the power lottery."

A short huff of laughter escaped me despite myself.

If only he knew.

"But that's not all."

"Of course, there's more," Bryce said flatly. I ignored him.

"My power doesn't just allow me to turn into a monster," I continued, letting my gaze pass over all four of them, making sure I had their attention.

Robert caught on instantly. "You can transform others as well."

"Whoa, whoa—" Kelly said, sitting forward now. "You can give people powers?" She hissed the words out, eyes wide.

That reaction was fair. From what I'd gathered, Parahuman abilities couldn't be artificially induced; they only manifested through traumatic, accidental circumstances.

By the way, that had been a bitch and a half to find, and I honestly only found out through Pritt when she caught me looking through her phone.

"Fucking hell," Bryce breathed, eyes wide, before narrowing slightly as tension crept into his posture. "This isn't some Teacher bullshit, is it?"

That name meant absolutely nothing to me.

The Narrative, as always, filled in the gaps.

Teacher: a Parahuman capable of granting others Thinker and Tinker abilities through touch, with the downside that recipients became susceptible to his manipulation.

Ah. Yeah. That would explain the concern.

Unfortunately, there was nothing I could say or show that would definitively reassure them. All I had was my word.

So I leaned into Leadership, into the Oni's straightforward nature, and spoke with nothing but honesty.

"No."

"Alright."

Well. That was easy.

"Way too easy!" Kelly remarked, shooting Bryce a look.

"Listen," Bryce said, crossing his arms as he leaned back, looking unusually thoughtful, "at the end of the day, this guy stared down the end of a gun's barrel for us. I say he's earned a little trust."

"…I really don't like it when you make sense, it feels gross."

Bryce shot her a glare. She responded with a grin full of teeth.

"But who will you give this to?" Robert asked. His expression hadn't shifted at all, even as the others bickered, his gaze steady and unwavering.

If this were anyone else, I might've thought he was pressuring me to choose him. Knowing Robert, though, it was just his face.

"The four of you."

"All of us?" Mr. Hong asked, surprise breaking through his composure as Bryce and Kelly turned toward me in shock.

"Yes."

For a second, none of them seemed to know what to say. The silence stretched just long enough to get uncomfortable before Mr. Hong let out a soft, amused laugh and pushed himself up from the table.

"Robert," he asked casually, already turning toward the cabinets lining the wall, "do you happen to have any sake here?"

My boss raised an eyebrow, watching him rummage through shelves like he owned the place. "On the right," he said after a moment, nodding toward a higher cabinet. "There's a good bottle there. Haven't opened it yet."

Without hesitation, the jolly sage retrieved it, along with five small cups, setting them down with deliberate care. One for each of us.

Even as he began pouring, the room felt strange, all of us watching him with varying degrees of confusion as the clear liquid filled each cup. The faint, sharp scent hit my nose almost immediately, and my Oni instincts perked up in a way that was deeply unhelpful.

"Hohoho," Mr. Hong chuckled, settling back into his seat as if this were all perfectly normal. "You see, since Mr. Rodrigues is being so generous with us, how about we observe a tradition that reflects the origins of his power?"

"Sake…" I muttered, eyes glued to the cup in front of me. "A Japanese tradition?"

I wanted to drink it. Badly. The smell alone had my mouth watering, and it took a frankly unreasonable amount of willpower not to grab the bottle and down it outright.

"Indeed," Mr. Hong said pleasantly. "It is called Sakazuki. A ritual of initiation, used to forge connections within groups and gatherings."

For some reason, he shot a knowing look toward Bryce and Kelly. Both of them were already grinning as they picked up their cups, far too eager for my liking, but I was occupied with other things.

"And," he continued, voice calm that I should have seen through it if my mind wasn't focused on the alcohol, "it is especially important when one presents oneself before their leader."

My brain stalled.

"What?" I asked flatly.

"Indeed, it's used even today back in the cities of Japan as a form of communion, a ritual to form bonds between subject and leader."

"Yes," Mr. Hong went on, completely unfazed. "It is still practiced today in certain circles in Japan, a form of communion meant to solidify bonds between leader and subject."

"Hold on," I tried, my eyebrow twitching as alarms went off in my head.

Unfortunately, no one paid me the slightest bit of attention.

I stared at the four people across from me with a dead, hollow expression, which they returned with matching grins that were very clearly at my expense. Then, as if they had rehearsed this ahead of time, all four of them raised their glasses.

"A toast to our newest bossman!" Bryce announced

"Banzai!" Mr Hong cheered along.

"Yooho!" Kelly rallied alongside him.

"Hmm," Robert simply hummed approvingly.

As they chugged down their drinks, I could only blink down at my own cup, filled with Sake.

Bob.

I know this is somehow your fucking fault.


[FEAT: Became the Villainous Leader of a Gang!

Fix this shit.

|Uncommon Trait|

Your genes grant you increased physical abilities, greater physical growth, and better combat instincts. You can also use the skill "Removal", which removes the natural limiters the brain has to stop you from using all 100% of your physical strength in order not to hurt yourself. You can increase your removal percentage by training.

​
…Okay then.

Am I just becoming some kind of mixed baby made out of every special bloodline in existence? First Rodrigues, now Kure, and this one's Japanese too.

Still, this came with a ridiculous number of upsides. Not for a normal human—because I crossed that line ages ago—but for my boosted Rodrigues form and my Oni transformation? This was huge.

Ignoring physical limiters without worrying too much about self-damage, thanks to regeneration?

Yeah. I'll take that.

[Dark Eater Midir]

|Elite Familiar|

Dark Souls - A descendant everlasting dragon that was raised by the gods and was tasked by Gwyn, the lord of sunlight to forever fight against the Abyss with its immortality, but eventually, it was corrupted by the Abyss in its eternal duty. While having lost his immortality, Midir is still very powerful and is capable of unleashing breaths of flame and beams of dark magic in addition to his giant and powerful body. Install Card Modification: Ally gains two phases. Full transformation allows complete manifestation as Midir.

Hahahahaha!

How fucking ironic.

I'm gearing up to fight a dragon, and the Gacha hands me a dragon as an ally.

That's actually hilarious.

Now the real question is… who would even be a good fit for something like this?

Eh. That's a problem for later. I'll figure it out as I go; I've always been pretty good at improvising.

Right now, my main concern is dealing with the four people who just collectively decided I was their leader.

Time to get showy.

"Very well," I laugh lightly, and if nothing else, that finally snaps their attention fully onto me.

Honestly, I just wanted to get this over with. The faster we did it, the faster we could move on to actual planning. Unfortunately, Leadership had other ideas, nudging at the back of my mind and insisting that this needed spectacle. If I was going to empower them, I had to make them feel chosen, make the moment stick, make it something they would remember every time things got rough.

So, theatrics it is.

"You four are the first," I say, spreading my hands slightly as I speak, my partially transformed Oni voice rolling out deeper and smoother than usual. "My enforcers, my hands, and my advisors."

Their attention sharpens, eyes tracking me, shoulders straightening almost without realizing it.

"The generals!" I add, clapping my hands together once.

When I pull them apart, several Install Cards shimmer into existence, floating in a slow orbit around the table, each one glowing faintly with intricate artwork depicting the familiar sealed inside. It's unnecessary flair, sure, but if the looks on their faces are anything to go by, it's doing exactly what Leadership wanted.

Awe has a way of cementing loyalty.

"Mr. Hong," I continue, and one of the cards drifts forward, separating itself from the rest. "For you."

The Install Card depicting Jigglypuff glides toward him, and he rises slightly from his chair to meet it, carefully taking the card between his fingers as though it might vanish if handled too roughly. The moment he touches it, I feel the familiar resonance again, the same subtle feedback I felt when Rachel bonded with hers. Jigglypuff's presence is warm and cheerful, like it's greeting him with genuine enthusiasm.

Yeah. Familiars are definitely still conscious inside the Install Cards.

They don't seem unhappy about it, though, so I'll chalk that up as a win.

"This…" Mr. Hong starts, his voice catching slightly as he studies the card.

"Hahahahaha!" Bryce cuts in, leaning forward with an unrestrained laugh. "It's a ball of fluff!"

I can't even blame him for that reaction.

Mr. Hong, however, ignores Bryce entirely, his brows knitting together as he turns the card slowly in his hands, lips parting as he licks his dried lips.

"The monster within this card," he says carefully, "is alive, isn't it?"

Oh. That's new.

Rachel never mentioned anything about that, but then again, if Hellhound talked to her, she probably wouldn't bring it up unless I asked directly.

Bryce's laughter dies instantly.

"Alive?" Kelly says flatly, waving a hand in front of her face like she's trying to brush the idea away. "Oi, don't say creepy shit like that."

"More or less," I nod toward Mr. Hong.

"Huh?!" Kelly's face crumples in real time, and I'll admit, that's kind of funny.

"Uh—hey, I'm sorry," Bryce blurts out, leaning closer to Mr. Hong while facing the card. "I shouldn't have laughed, that was a dick move."

The card flashes briefly, a soft twinkle of light, and Mr. Hong smiles faintly as he feels it.

"I believe it forgives you," he says calmly.

Bryce and Kelly both visibly stiffen and break out in cold sweat, the realization clearly sinking in that their cards will probably come with conscious passengers too.

"Is it sentient?" Robert asks, his expression unchanged, eyes steady on me.

It's a good question.

As far as I knew, probably not. From what I gathered of this feedback, Familiars should have been capable of summoning sentient things from across the multiverse, but upon being turned into Install Cards, I can easily feel the change; they are still sentient, but seemed to have been programmed with only the goal of helping their users.

"Think of them as partners," I explain, shifting my weight slightly as I speak. "They have limited sentience, but their primary purpose is to support their user. They adapt to you, not the other way around."

That seems to settle him.

"What is her name?" Mr. Hong asks, tilting the card slightly as if listening for a response.

…Did he just say her?

Guess he's already synced with it better than expected.

"Jigglypuff," I reply, keeping my face perfectly straight.

Bryce nearly loses it again, clapping a hand over his mouth while Kelly snorts despite herself.

"She can generate powerful sonic attacks," I continue, unfazed, "and put anyone who hears her song into a deep sleep. Just be careful, the stronger the enemy, the more energy it costs."

"Damn," Kelly mutters, staring at the card with newfound respect. "I take it back. That thing sounds terrifying."

And she's not wrong.

The ability to disable entire groups without firing a shot is absurdly strong, especially against normal people. Against parahumans it might struggle depending on resistances, but in a street-level fight? Mr. Hong just became one of the most dangerous men in the room.

"I believed that for you," I say, looking back to Mr. Hong, "someone who values peaceful solutions, the ability to end a fight without unnecessary harm would matter."

Leadership hums in quiet approval at the thought. Taking your subordinates' desires into account, showing that you understand them and have chosen for them rather than over them, was how trust became something unshakable.

"To use her power," I continue, "simply say 'Install.'"

Mr. Hong inclines his head in a small, respectful bow, fingers curling around the card with surprising reverence.

"For you two, the power is similar," I say, turning my attention toward Bryce and Kelly. A single Install Card splits cleanly in two midair, the image of the Bell Gargoyles separating into mirrored forms. Originally only supposed to be given to two of them, but I could now give it to six different people if I wanted to.

Hmm, with a bit of focus on the surplus of unused Install Cards at my disposal, I can afford to refine things. Weaving the unused cards into the originals, reinforcing them, strengthening what was already there instead of spreading it thin.

"Gargoyles," I explain, guiding one card toward Bryce with a flick of my wrist, "your bodies will become living stone, granting you immense strength and speed."

The second card floats toward Kelly.

"One of you breathes fire," I say, nodding toward Bryce, "and the other breathes lightning."

Both of them stare at the cards hovering inches from their hands, expression a mix of excitement and disbelief.

"And for you, Robert," I add, unable to stop the grin that creeps onto my face, "there really wasn't another option."

The Iron Golem card surges forward, cutting through the air in a straight line toward him. Originally, I could summon up to four of these, but now that number has doubled. Instead of distributing them, I collapse every instance into a single card, compressing their power into something far denser, far more durable.

Robert snatches it out of the air with practiced ease, eyes narrowing slightly as he studies the image.

"I thought it would fit," I shrug. "The Iron Golem is similar to the Gargoyles, but without elemental attacks. In exchange, your defence is absurd, something on another level. On top of that, most parahuman abilities will have a much harder time affecting you."

Robert nods along with my words, not minding about his lack of firepower. I can already see the hundreds of ways he's thinking about how to use such enhanced endurance in a fight. Grappling will probably be his best bet; a prolonged fight will be the nightmare of any enemy.

I give them a few minutes after that, letting them adjust and quietly connect with their new partners. Watching grown criminals poke, prod, and tentatively converse with glowing cards is… honestly kind of surreal.

Eventually, it's Robert who breaks the lull.

"Then there's only one thing left," he says, folding his arms.

"What do we call ourselves?"

"Well," Bryce shrugs, leaning back in his chair, "that's the boss's job, isn't it?"

"Just don't make it corny," Kelly adds flatly.

"I believe we should lean into the monster theme we seem to have going," Mr. Hong offers mildly.

I close my eyes.

A name. Something that could stand against the ABB, the Empire, Coil, and anyone else who thought they could carve us out of this city. A name that reflected the protection that I wanted to give, the name that inflicted fear into the enemies that dared go against it.

A name that will show what we are.

In my chest, the Oni heart begins to beat, thrumming, cheering, like a child that got to see its old friends once again after a long time.

"Our gang is built on the ideals of the Night's Rest." My voice is steady, even as something bubbles behind it, a fire that didn't feel wholly mine yet carried through with me.

"Those who share that ideal will carry the name of the Night."

I open my eyes.

"Yes," I continue, the conviction settling fully into my bones, "we are—"

The name that I never heard yet knew from the bottom of my soul.

"The Night Parade of One Hundred Demons."
