#650
POV: Kellista Lee
Date: While Samuel is taking a walk in a picnic.

What the fuck is she doing?

No, scratch that—she knew exactly what she was doing.

The real question was why.

Kellista had always been simple when it came to decisions. If she wanted something, she did it. If the money was good enough, she did it. If the benefits outweighed the risks, she definitely did it.

And if something smelled like it might come back to bite her in the ass later?

She didn't touch it.

That was what brought her right back to the same damn question.

What the fuck was she doing?

"Move it, people!" Kelly barked, her voice echoing sharply through the hollow interior of the warehouse. "We've got shit to prep, and I don't see enough wheels burning rubber in here!"

The effect was immediate.

Her people hurried between trucks and stacked crates, dragging out stage equipment, banners, lighting rigs, anything that had been scraped together from whatever stores were still open and willing to sell without asking too many questions.

It looked messy.

Scratch that, it was messy.

But it was getting done.

The abandoned warehouse they were using as a staging ground sat in one of the worst parts of Downtown, a broken thing with shattered windows and rusted metal that was depressingly common in Brockton Bay, not worth fixing because nobody cared enough to try.

This shit smelled bad, dusty as hell, and the sound was off because of how empty it had been. At least now, with everything being moved in it was better.

This was where they were setting up for a rally.

That alone almost made her laugh.

When was the last time she'd even been to a rally?

…Had she ever?

Kelly frowned slightly as she leaned against one of the metal beams, arms crossed, while her eyes tracked the constant motion of her crew.

Her gang had never been that kind of operation.

They were small—just a handful of drivers running jobs, moving product, keeping control over a few key underground racing routes where the majority of the money came from.

There wasn't much need for speeches or grandstanding bullshit. Just engines cutting through the air, staying ahead of anyone stupid enough to try and take their slice, and simultaneously being quiet enough that none of the cape gangs decided to squash them.

And before even that?

She'd been a solo merc.

So yeah, rallies weren't exactly her thing.

Honestly, the whole concept used to confuse the hell out of her.

How the fuck could a bunch of members from villainous gangs gather in one place, and watch as their Villain bosses stand on a stage, and start preaching to a crowd without the Protectorate dropping on their heads the moment it happened?

It didn't make sense.

Not until she learned it wasn't about logic.

Because Cape culture often fucking wasn't.

As far as anyone official was concerned, tomorrow was just some kind of community event. What kind? Didn't matter. Hong was handling that part, and Kelly trusted him to spin whatever bullshit was needed to make it sound legitimate.

On her end?

She told her people they were getting paid to move equipment and manage logistics, which kept the information quiet on her end.

Of course, that kind of thin cover didn't actually fool anyone important. The cops weren't blind, and the Protectorate sure as hell wasn't stupid. When multiple gangs started drifting toward the same location, people noticed.

But that wasn't the point.

The point was plausible deniability.

A flimsy excuse both sides could grab onto, so nobody had to be the first to escalate.

Still, they're the Protectorate, right? Why the fuck would they not hit a major soft spot like that, even if the villains decide to escalate? It would still be a huge win on their end!

Kelly exhaled slowly through her nose, her gaze narrowing slightly as she watched a group struggle to haul a lighting rig into place.

Because hitting a rally wasn't just about breaking it up.

It was a statement.

It told every gang in the city that the Protectorate wouldn't allow them to gather, recruit, or show strength in public.

And that came with consequences, because unlike normal people, Capes can be truly destructive.

Because if the Protectorate started crashing rallies, then every other gang would start looking at their public events the same way. Charity drives, notable appearances, patrols routes—anything visible became fair game.

Nobody wanted that kind of escalation.

Not unless they were damn sure they could handle the fallout.

Because Cape culture, at the end of the day, came down to one thing.

Reputation. Both from villains and from heroes.

It was how Lung managed to rule through fear despite being a lazy bastard who barely left his territory, and how the Empire held onto nearly a third of the city with their matching outfits and bullshit ideology.

So at the end of the day, Cape culture was about swinging your dick, and the biggest one won.

And here in Brockton Bay? The Protectorate ENE didn't.

Which was exactly why they leaned on plausible deniability so hard.

At the end of the day, it gave everyone an easy out. The Protectorate could shrug, shake their heads, and say there was nothing they could have, while the gangs could pretend they weren't doing anything illegal in the first place.

A neat little lie everyone agreed to look over.

Of course, that doesn't mean the Protectorate will do nothing. Kelly wasn't fucking stupid.

With this many people gathering in one place, there were going to be eyes everywhere. Dozens of plants are easy. Some from the gangs, some from the Protectorate, watching everything and reporting back to someone else.

At least the short planning time for this rally would ensure there wouldn't be as many plants as there could have been.

In fact, having spies had been part of Flatline's plan from the start.

Most people would be cautious of having so many spies in their confines, but her newest boss wasn't one of them. In fact, he welcomed them, because it meant that his message would spread even further than just this.

Yeah, no doubt about it, Flatline definitely had a fat cock. Leah was a lucky girl, at least if she locks him up already, if Kelly has to watch another day of Leah stumbling around her crush, she'll force them into a closet herself.

Kelly let out a quiet, humorless chuckle as she pushed herself off the beam, rolling her shoulders as she looked over the growing setup around her.

She was trying to distract herself, but her thoughts kept circling back to the absurdity of the situation.

"And now I'm part of this shit," Kelly muttered under her breath.

She was the one responsible for moving everything to this damn warehouse, organizing the logistics, and making sure all this junk actually got where it needed to go on time. Her people were the only ones fast enough and reliable enough to pull it off on such short notice.

And she was doing it for free.

For. Fucking. Free.

"Uuuuuugh…" she groaned quietly, dragging a hand down her face.

Once again, the same question circled back around.

What the fuck was she doing?

This time, though, the answer came from a pulse.

A low, steady warmth spreading somewhere deep inside her chest, not physical in the way she was used to, but close enough that her brain tried to categorize it the same way anyway.

Some might have called it invasive, but connected as Kelly was to the source, she couldn't help but simply feel how comforting and reassuring it was.

As if someone was checking in to make sure that she was okay.

…Concerned.

Kelly let out a short, incredulous huff.

"Are you serious right now?" she muttered under her breath.

Fuck's sake, since when are Gargoyles empathetic? Aren't these guys made of stone or something?

Normally, a cape that could hand out power like cheap candy the way Flatline did would have her taking a step back.

Especially one who came with what truly felt like personalities and feelings.

This was the kind of shit that got people killed.

Except that, in the bond that she shared, Kelly could truly feel a connection towards the Gargoyle, a two-way street that was deeper than anything she had ever had.

Fuck, that's even more concerning!

…so again, what the fuck was she doing?

Kelly's gaze drifted back to her crew, watching them move in coordinated bursts of activity, hauling crates, assembling structures, setting everything up piece by piece. They finally set up the proper stage at this point, and she noted how the majority of the cars were much lighter now.

Her mind began to wander.

Her early life isn't exactly something worth reminiscing over; a drunk dad and an absent mom didn't make for good combinations.

Running away hadn't made things better either. Being a small, weak girl out on the streets just meant trading one kind of danger for another, going from one roof to another, a man simply grabbing her off the streets.

Except this time the guy wasn't related to her, so he fucked her a few times.

It took longer than she liked to admit before she found the nerve to push back against the bastard who thought he owned her.

In the end, it hadn't even been complicated. She grabbed a gun after he used her, slept, and with his guard down, it had been easy. She aimed at his head and pulled the trigger.

Problem solved.

That was the day she learned something important. A good gun could turn even a weak, scared girl into something dangerous.

There hasn't been a day since that she hasn't carried one.

Not long after that, Behemoth hit a nearby city, and the world went to shit in the way it always did when an Endbringer showed up. Amid the chaos, barely out of puberty and already done with everything, she joined one of the mass migrations heading toward the States, smuggling herself in.

From there, it was just mercenary work on her plate.

Young girl like her? Easy pickings, is what they all thought. So Kelly made them all become very acquainted with her guns, and a name for herself was made.

Mercenary work was tough, but it paid the bills while keeping her alive.

She hated it.

Kelly hated it so much it hurt. Being constantly on the lookout for other guns for hire, looking over her shoulder every moment, always trying to find work, never enough, running from authorities when things got hot.

Even after it became normal, after she adapted to everything.

She hated it.

If there was one good thing to come out of it, though…

It was her bike.

An old thing that she bought off and then had to fix all on her godamn own. Which she did!

The wind in her face, her hair whipping behind her as the engine roared beneath her, the feeling of speed and control and freedom that nothing else in her life had ever managed to give her.

That was what she loved.

Before she knew it, she was entering underground races, winning more often than she lost, picking up parts, and accidentally making connections. A few idiots started following her around, both offering help and looking for guidance – in her, of all people!

And somehow, without her really noticing when it happened, that turned into a gang.

That part had scared the shit out of her.

Kelly would be the first to admit she was absolute garbage when it came to relationships. Between her personality and the fact that she didn't exactly put in the effort to maintain anything, it wasn't surprising.

Hell, she didn't even care about people.

Another pulse, the same comforting warmth flowing through a bond.

"…Yeah, yeah," she muttered under her breath, not quite looking at anything in particular. "I'm fine."

The presence didn't leave.

…No.

If she was being honest with herself, and she hated doing that, it wasn't that she didn't care about others, or that she couldn't handle relationships like some kind of clueless idiot who scared her men off.

She was bad with people because she was fucking terrified of them.

Not of what they could do to her physically, not anymore at least, but of everything else that came with getting close to someone, attachments, the slow, creeping feeling that you might actually start to care about someone enough that losing them would hurt.

Kelly went through it once, and it almost broke her.

So she didn't let it happen again.

She pushed people away before they could get close. She snapped, mocked, insulted, and made herself so unpleasant that no normal person would stick around long enough to matter.

It worked.

It always worked.

…Except now it didn't.

Because despite all that, despite her attitude and her complete lack of effort to be anything resembling approachable, these idiots still rallied behind her. And whether she liked it or not, Kelly felt a reluctant responsibility for them.

Which is why she didn't just turn tail the moment they started this fight with the ABB.

Looking after these knuckleheads, it had been an exhausting year.

It was honestly more work than she ever did, work that she didn't even sign up for!

But Kelly couldn't lie to herself here, that she didn't feel anything towards them.

That she didn't feel anything towards Night's Rest.

For those loud, annoying assholes who laughed with her, who joked with her like she was one of them, who took her insults and threw them right back without missing a beat.

The same bastards she kicked down when they got too cocky, the same idiots who never let her forget that her mouth was filthier than a sailor's and that she somehow still couldn't keep a man around.

And…

Her jaw tightened slightly.

She especially couldn't lie about that part.

The fact that, for the first time in her life…

Someone had protected her.

She didn't know what the hell she was feeling.

This warmth that came from the creature now tied to her, and the other warmth, one that had nothing to do with powers or whatever the hell Flatline had given her.

But she wasn't stupid.

If someone gave her something like that—

If someone had her back like that—

Then she'd return the favor.

Simple as that.

"C'mon, you bastards!" Kelly shouted suddenly, her voice snapping through the warehouse as she swung herself onto her bike in one smooth motion, the engine roaring to life beneath her as the heavily modified machine vibrated with barely restrained power. "We're racing against death!"

Around her, her people cheered.

Kelly couldn't help the grin that spread across her face as she leaned forward slightly, revving the engine just enough to feel it rumble.

What a weird fucking life.

At no point had she ever imagined she'd be gearing up to wage war against a supervillain's gang.

Or that she'd be doing it as part of one.

Flatline had to be completely insane to pull something like this off, to look her in the eye and convince her to go along with it using that calm, unfazed attitude of his.

It was kind of cute.

Kelly huffed out a short laugh.

If her younger self could see her now, she'd probably say something like, 'You're bending over for a kid?' right before putting a bullet in her head for good measure.

A flash of light interrupted the thought. The card surged forward from within her, appearing in her waiting hand as it belonged there, the Bell Gargoyle etched into its surface seeming almost alive as it gave a nod.

Kellista Lee grinned.

Well, it made sense.

After all, who else could make her bend but a true —


POV: Bryce Rolando
Date: While Samuel is eating dinner with a hound.

The world was made up of roles.

Bryce had believed that for a very long time, and the same time hadn't done anything to shake that belief. If anything, the years had only proven it right, reinforcing the idea over and over again until it became something he could rely on no matter where he was or who he was dealing with.

Everyone had a place.

Everyone had a role.

"Jack! You spread the word?" Bryce asked, stepping forward and clasping the man's hand in a firm shake, thought it was more familiarity than formality in here.

They were standing inside a mechanic's shop, the place mostly empty except for a small group of guys gathered near the entrance, talking among themselves while occasionally glancing over.

Jack, the man in front of him, matched Bryce in size, his face carrying that same easy-going smile he always wore like nothing in the world could really bother him.

"Yup," Jack replied without hesitation. "Just like you asked. Wasn't exactly discreet, though, with how short the notice is."

Bryce waved that off.

"Nah, that's fine. We just need everything ready for tomorrow."

Jack hummed in acknowledgment, but didn't press further.

And that right there was why Bryce liked him so much.

Jack knew his role.

He followed orders, did what was asked, and didn't complicate things by trying to be more than what he was needed for.

A Servant.

Which was not in a bad way of the description, just the way that things were.

Back when he was still a kid, back in Juárez, his mom used to tell him stories at night. It was her way of keeping him calm, of distracting him from the sounds that echoed through the streets, the gunshots and the shouting.

They lived in one of the poorer parts of town, right where the Juárez cartel and the Sinaloa cartel liked to clash over territory, and violence wasn't something rare.

After a while, the gunfire stopped being something to fear and started becoming something else entirely.

A signal.

A quiet little cue that told him his mom would be coming soon to tell him more fantastical stories that she would come up with.

And she always did.

His mother was… everything, back then.

A beautiful woman who worked herself to exhaustion just to keep them afloat, taking whatever jobs she could find, most often as a waitress, earning just enough to cover the bills, and the money they had to hand over so nobody came knocking at their door with bad intentions.

It wasn't a good life.

But it was life.

And with the worlds that she showed him through her stories, she made it worth living.

Stories about heroes who stood against evil, about thieves clever enough to steal entire kingdoms, about loyal servants who protected their lords with unwavering devotion, and warriors who fought monsters that threatened everything they cared about.

And every time she spoke, Bryce made sure to listen closely.

His young mind soaked it all in, each story leaving something behind, little fragments that slowly came together into something bigger.

Roles.

Heroes, villains, servants, warriors, monsters, comedians, friends, lovers.

With every story, his world became easier to understand because now he had a way to categorize it, a way to look at people and place them somewhere that made sense.

And it worked, which was why he kept on doing it.

Even his mom encouraged it, smiling softly whenever he tried to explain his thoughts to her, telling him that if he was going to follow someone one day, then he had to make sure they were worth it.

"A true King," she had said once, brushing a hand through his hair, "Someone who cares for their people, who protects them, who deserves to be followed."

"Someone who cares for their people, who protects them, who deserves to be followed."

Bryce had promised her he would find someone like that.

She died a few weeks later.

Apparently, she had been a block away when a shootout broke out between the cartels and the police, and that was all it took.

A stray bullet took her life. Dead after bleeding out on the ground, left lung pierced through.

Bryce still remembered the moment they took her away.

The way her hand looked so pale. He remembered standing there, and his mind going black as everything else faded into the background.

He doesn't remember if he cried.

When the police tried to approach him, he ran as fast as he could, not trusting them, not wanting to be placed under someone else's care.

And with nowhere else to go, no one else to turn to, he fell into the only thing that made sense at the time.

Work for the cartel.

He started carrying things—contraband, or packages—hiding them in his clothes, using the fact that he was just a kid to slip past attention that would have stopped an adult. The police didn't look too closely.

It was supposed to be temporary, just something to get by. He had promised himself that.

He promised that the runner job was just a thing until he could get something more stable as an adult.

But the kind of life he stepped into wasn't the kind you just walked away from.

It wrapped around you slowly, pulling you deeper and deeper until you didn't even realize how far in you'd gone until it was too late to leave without severe repercussions.

But it wasn't all lost.

Because within the system that existed inside the cartel, Bryce learned.

And more importantly, he understood.

There was a structure to everything, even in something as chaotic as crime, and once he started looking at it through the lens of roles, the entire thing began to make sense in a way that nothing else ever had before.

He learned how deals were made, not just in the words spoken but in the pauses between them, in the way tone shifted when someone wanted to apply pressure without making it obvious. He learned where threats were most effective, when to push and when to step back, and how easily the right phrase or a folded bill slipping between fingers could make authority look the other way.

The deeper he went, the more the structure revealed itself.

The accountants controlled the money and, by extension, a surprising amount of power behind the scenes, while the enforcers protected both the product and the people who mattered. The dealers were something else entirely, twisting their words into whatever shape was needed to close a sale or defuse a situation before it turned ugly.

And through all of it, Bryce kept categorizing.

More and more roles were put in the framework of his mind.

Some were servants, following orders without question. Others were warriors, built for conflict and little else, while a rare few stood out as something closer to commanders, people who could guide others instead of just acting on instinct.

Work at the cartel, even though his labelling of roles, wasn't easy

There wasn't much space for friendships in that kind of environment, at least not the kind Bryce would have wanted, and that naturally pushed him into the position of an outsider more often than not.

He didn't mind.

If anything, it suited him, even if it made things harder.

Being on the outside meant he could learn more without being pulled into the mess of emotions and loyalties that seemed to complicate everything for everyone else.

For a younger Bryce, that was more than enough.

Time passed.

Years, really.

And eventually, after working his way up through the ranks and proving himself reliable enough to be trusted with more responsibility, he finally got the chance to meet the one person he had been curious about from the very beginning.

The boss.

By that point, Bryce had already carved out a place for himself, no longer just a runner or a background piece, but someone who led others, someone who could be relied on to handle problems without constant supervision.

Still, the idea of meeting the man at the top stirred deep-seated excitement in him.

The kind of anticipation that made his thoughts race as he tried to imagine what role someone like that would fill.

A leader of a cartel, a cape powerful enough to stand against rival organizations and maintain control over his territory, someone who had built and held onto power in a place where weakness was punished immediately…

That kind of person had to be something special.

He had to be.

But when they finally stood face to face, when their eyes met, and Bryce got his first real look at the man he had spent years indirectly working under…

He felt nothing, just disappointment.

Because the man in front of him wasn't a King.

He was greedy, driven by nothing more than money and the influence it could buy, clinging to power not because he deserved it, but because he refused to let it go.

There were no further visions of where to go next, no sense of responsibility toward the people under him, no indication that he was anything more than a man who had climbed high enough to take what he wanted and would remain seated because he was comfortable.

Not someone worth fighting for.

Not someone worth dying for.

Bryce left soon after.

His standing within the cartel was high enough by that point that they trusted him to walk away without immediately turning into a liability, even if he understood perfectly well that if anything ever traced back to him, there would be consequences waiting.

Still, it was a clean enough break.

From Mexico, he moved to the States, and from there he drifted between different gangs, different groups, different opportunities, his experience making him valuable enough that he never struggled to find work.

More than once, he found himself amused when the Protectorate looked into his background, piecing together the trail he had left behind, it was quite extensive.

Through all of it, he kept searching for someone worth following.

Granted, it turned out to be much harder than he expected it.

Over the course of nearly fifteen years, he had met plenty of leaders, each one impressive in their own way. Some had a way with words that could sway entire rooms, others commanded respect through sheer competence, and a few had that intangible presence that made people want to listen without really knowing why.

But none of them could go beyond that, none of them had the qualities he was looking for.

So eventually, Bryce decided to try it himself.

If he couldn't find someone worth following, then maybe he could become that person, at least to some degree.

Brockton Bay gave him that chance.

A small opportunity, nothing grand, but enough to build something of his own and see how far he could take it.

It didn't last.

Because in a world where power mattered as much as it did, not being a cape put a hard limit on how far he could go, no matter how well he understood the structure of things.

And in the end, that limitation caught up to him.

So he let it go.

He settled into his own role as a mediocre leader, a man who was charming enough to gather a large following of people who had been rejected by others. All while ignoring the need to go and chase an ideal that might not even exist.

Even as the promise to his other lingered in the back of his mind.

So, he remained here, in Brockton Bay, resigning himself to his own role.

And it wasn't so bad. It was fun to joke around with Hong and Kelly, to have no worries when he stepped into the Night's Rest.

But then—

Finally.

After all that time, after all those years of searching and settling and almost giving up on the idea entirely.

He found it.

With a pulse, a card found itself within his pockets, comfortably inside the palm of his hand, he felt the power coursing through it.

Bryce had found someone worth following.

A true—


POV: Xiou Hong
Date: While Samuel is dicking down a hound.

Xiou Hong believed that to pity others was easy.

It came naturally to most people, rising without effort when they saw suffering laid bare in front of them. The feeling itself was not rare, nor was the quiet thought that followed, the voice that suggested something should be done.

What was rare was action.

Because more often than not, people simply did nothing.

Xiou Hong had done the same.

Throughout his life, he had seen many things that stirred that fleeting sense of pity within him, yet for some reason or another, he did nothing.

Sometimes it was because events moved too quickly, accidents resolved, or lives lost before he even became aware of them, leaving behind nothing but the vague knowledge that something unfortunate had occurred.

Other times it was far more personal, like passing by someone on the street whose circumstances were clearly worse than his own, and thinking, briefly, that he should help, only to realize he had nothing to give.

And then there were moments that truly made him ashamed, in a different way. Moments where he felt nothing at all, only apathy as he observed suffering that by all accounts, should have moved him in some way.

Those were the moments he remembered the most.

Because they forced him to confront something uncomfortable about himself, something he could not easily justify or dismiss.

In China, under the authoritative rule of the C.U.I, life for the average citizen was stable. There was not much to say, outside that for those who did not live in the system, it would appear for all manners to be orderly, maybe even normal.

The government maintained a tight grip on everything, and in return, they provided a sense of security that people grew accustomed to, a structure that made daily life feel safe.

The Yangban stood as symbols of that protection, powerful figures who embodied strength and unity, and the people looked to them with pride.

From the outside, it was easy to believe that everything was as it should be.

But stability came with a cost.

Because while the majority lived their lives within that carefully maintained order, there were others who existed beyond its reach, pushed into the margins where their struggles were no longer visible to the wider population and then ignored by any help that could be given.

The poorest regions were not hidden in the sense that they did not exist, but rather in the way they were ignored, quietly excluded from the narrative that the C.U.I presented to its people.

The philosophy of the C.U.I was simple. If you exclude 10% of the colony, the bottom, those who do not contribute enough to society, then they will be ignored and treated as garbage. Do that, and the 80% of the working class will make sure to work enough to never fall to the bottom, while the 10% at the top – the C.U.I – reap the benefits.

It was disgusting, as it was deplorable.

Xiou Hong had been part of the 80%, and as a younger man, he had felt for them in the same distant way he felt for many things, acknowledging their hardships without allowing those thoughts to disrupt the flow of his 'normal' life.

There was always a reason not to act, always a justification that made inaction seem reasonable in the moment.

But knowing something existed and truly understanding it were two very different things.

And it wasn't until Leviathan came to Shanghai that everything changed.

The Chinese Union-Imperial loved to portray itself as absolute, but there is very little that it can do when an Endbringer comes knocking. And the illusion that nothing truly catastrophic could reach Xiou was broken.

The attack did not feel real at first. It actually felt like the stories or distant reports of disaster. It wasn't until he saw his destroyed home that it truly sank in, because this was not something happening somewhere else, to people he would never meet.

This was happening to him.

To everyone around him.

The controlled world he had known was torn apart in a matter of moments, replaced by chaos so overwhelming that there was no time to process it properly, or space to fall back on the detached calm he had carried for so long.

For the first time in his life, Xiou Hong understood what it meant to need help.

An immediate, desperate feeling.

And when that realization came, so did another.

Help was not guaranteed.

Xiou Hong saw it then, what it meant to reach out, to hope for salvation, and to have that hope pass over you in favour of something else.

It was a despair unlike anything he had ever known.

To lose everything in such a short span of time was one thing, but what lingered far longer, what truly settled into his bones and refused to leave, was the realization that in the midst of all that loss, he had simply not mattered.

It had not been cruelty; he understood that now.

But understanding did not make it any easier to bear.

Because it forced him to confront the uncomfortable truth that, for most of his life, he had been on the other side of that equation, standing among those who could have done something, and choosing instead to walk away as if nothing had happened.

The thought followed him long after the waters receded, long after the ruins of Shanghai became irreparable, long after he joined the waves of displaced people moving toward somewhere—anywhere—that they could even go.

He carried it with him all the way to the United States.

The migration itself blurred together in his memory, a long and exhausting stretch of uncertainty where people clung to whatever scraps of hope they could find, moving alongside others who had lost just as much, if not more.

Remnants caused by Endbringer clashes of Kyushu, Jinzhou, and Shanghai all converged into a single current, pushing forward because staying behind was no longer an option.

Eventually, the current brought him to Brockton Bay.

It was not the kind of place people wished to end up in.

The city was a wreck, especially when he arrived, having just hosted perhaps the worst amount of villainous gangs in the entirety of the US, with the Marquis now behind bars, the Slaughterhouse Nine bounding off, and the rest consolidating their forces.

In that sense, it was incredibly different from the stability that he had known before.

This time, Xiou Hong did not look away.

This time, he made a promise to himself that he would help those in need, those whom he was capable of helping.

Because now he understood more than ever what it felt like to need it and not receive it.

The promise did not make him change immediately, nor was it perfect.

There were still moments where hesitation crept in, where old habits tried to assert themselves, where he questioned whether his efforts made any real difference in a place as complicated as this city.

Soon, experience tempered his intentions, reminding him that not every situation could be resolved so simply, that sometimes helping one person meant drawing the attention of others who might take advantage of that kindness.

Even so, he did not stop.

Because even a small act could matter.

Xiou Hong smiled softly as he walked through the streets, his calm almost out of place against the backdrop of Brockton Bay.

"Mr Hong!" To the side of the street, a vendor called him out, a young man who he helped settle street stalls to get money for his family, "You want a skewer? It's on the house!"

Xiou accepted it with a polite bow of his head, "Thank you."

Not long after, he passed a pair of children gathered near the edge of the street, their eyes lingering just a bit too long on the food in his hand.

He did not pause to think about it.

He simply knelt slightly, offering the skewer to them with that same gentle smile, watching as hesitation gave way to eager acceptance, the children lighting up.

"Thank you Mister," The older one, holding what Xiou believes to be his sibling my the hand, bowed to him, before both ran off.

There was worth in such acts, that even in a city as weighted down by its problems as Brockton Bay was, there were still opportunities to make things just a little better for someone else.

More than that, there was comfort in realising that he was not alone in that effort.

Because Brockton Bay, for all its darkness, was not without people who stood above it. It had individuals who carried burdens far greater than his own and still moved forward.

Xiou Hong had seen enough to recognize that.

And in that regard, he found himself grateful that someone like Samuel existed.

Grateful that, at an age where Xiou Hong himself had been content to look away, Samuel had already chosen to act, to step forward and shoulder responsibilities that many would forsake for how heavy it would be.

Their path was not one without danger; he understood that as well.

There would be consequences, and many would see what they are building as a target to knock it down.

But even so, Xiou Hong could not help but feel certain that things would be okay.

Because Samuel was a true-

POV: Samuel Rodrigrues

Date: 18th​ of March – Night

I focused on the steady rhythm of my own heartbeat as I kept my eyes closed and my breathing even.

It was almost time.

The room I was in was small, tucked away just behind the makeshift backstage of the refurbished warehouse, a forgotten space that hadn't been properly touched in years.

Dust clung to everything in a thin, suffocating layer, settling over old cleaning supplies stacked haphazardly in the corners. Those damn subordinates of mine, all subservient in one moment, then laughing as I opened the door to my 'office' with a flat face.

I sat in the middle of it all on a cold steel chair, arms crossed as I leaned back slightly, ignoring the environment entirely as I focused inward.

Most of my thoughts circled around what came next.

Not just the speech, but everything that would follow after it, because standing in front of a crowd and convincing them to follow me was one thing, but actually managing them afterwards would be something else entirely.

A gang could be simplified to simple muscle and numbers, but at the end of the day it was also much more than that if I wanted to compete on the big leagues. I needed to get structures set down after this, and check the people for their own wants and limits to be handled properly.

Thankfully I had that part mostly covered with Management.

Outside my little room, the warehouse was far from quiet. Even from here I could pick up the low hum of conversation, the occasional burst of laughter, and the steady movement of people shifting around the space.

Some of them were solo operators with just enough influence to matter, while others were leaders of smaller gangs that Bryce had convinced to show up on such short notice.

Right now, they were mingling under the illusion of a harmless community event - which no one truly believed - eating through whatever food Kelly's people had gathered, while Mr. Hong's connections kept the whole thing wrapped in just enough legitimacy to avoid immediate scrutiny.

It was almost impressive how smoothly it had all come together, if one didn't know about the Management that I had to do for all the logistics.

I had made it very clear to Bryce that I wasn't interested in just anyone joining us. I wanted people who actually aligned with what we were building, not just opportunists looking for an easy win.

Sure, forcing compliance was always an option if things didn't go my way, but that wasn't the kind of foundation I wanted to build on.

A forced ally was just a bomb waiting to go off.

Hmm.

I still had some time to kill.

Might as well see what the Gacha had for me.

Bronze first.

|Common Item|

This box contains 9 turrets whose bullet reserves refill every 24 hours(bullets cannot be taken out of turret). Restock Timer: 168 Hours

​
A grin broke out.

Fuck yeah.

I'm gonna need to bring this out in the right place, and sure setting them up would probably be a pain, but once they were in position, any area I controlled would immediately become a nightmare to invade.

Even if they weren't top-tier quality, nine automated guns that never really ran out of ammo were absolutely ridiculous.

Wait, holy shit they godamned restock?

Infinite Turrets?

…I might have a boner.

Well, until I figured out where to actually place them, into the soul space you go.

Allow you to turn your teeth into very sharp and durable fangs.

At least it's another ability. Three more and I'll get another slot if the pattern holds.

Let's try with Silver next then.

|Trash Item|

An otherwise ordinary and unremarkable head-sized rock that is always ever so slightly heavier than the maximum weight of whoever tries to lift it can lift.

​
Hmm, why the hell was this labelled as trash?

Because from where I was sitting, this was actually kind of amazing.

If someone was already down, all I had to do was place this on their chest, and that was it, they weren't going anywhere, the perfect restrain.

Though, I guess there were limitations.

Still, I won't be able to actually move it around physically.

It specifically said heavier than what I could lift, which meant I wouldn't be able to just pick it up and move it around normally. Pushing it might still work, since that wasn't the same as lifting, but that would depend on how the effect actually functioned in practice.

Either way, I wouldn't be carrying it around physically.

Which meant the process would be simple in theory but annoying in execution: take it out of my soul, use it, then touch it again to store it back.

That would get annoying fast.

Still useful though.

I have two advantage gold tickets, I hope at least one of them give me something good.

Spider-Man?

Who the hell was that? That name meant absolutely nothing to me. I would hate to actually meet a spider man, that shit sounds horrifying.

Spiders back home were not something to trifle with.

It added directly to my existing toolkit, giving me a lot more versatility, and another layer of elemental synergy to work with. Between fire, water, and now wind, I was starting to cover a pretty solid range of options.

All I needed now was earth to round things out.

Was lightning one of the main elements too?

An entire battlefield could be reshaped with that kind of power, blizzards rolling in on command could be used to freeze terrain and limit movement, all while allowing for the ice beams to control space. It was frankly an overwhelming type of advantage that could decide fights before they even started.

And I would have loved to have it.

But I couldn't afford to think like that.

I can heal easily enough with my passive regeneration of an Oni, never mind the absolute bullshit that was my enhanced alcohol-based recovery, where I could bounce back from death's door to top form with my energy renewed.

The more people I brought into this, the more responsibility I carried towards them. Powering them up wasn't just about making them stronger, it meant they would be on the front lines, they would be the piercing tip of the spear in most of our battles, which meant injuries more likely than not.

How many Parahumans could actually heal?

The Heroes had Panacea, which meant that even if they were in a disadvantage of almost having triple the villain count, they could bounce back from every engagement completely fresh after a hit from the Raccoon.

Healing was a massive force multiplier. Specially since capes were the main pillars of any organisation. Having someone who can keep the cape from collapsing by healing made a fundamental difference.

That much I knew.

As for how the villains dealt with it… I wasn't sure, but I had tried to look into it.

My new phone had made that possible, even if the process itself felt like I had willingly thrown myself into a labyrinth designed to waste as much of my time as possible.

The amount of information available was ridiculous, thousands of links branching into more links, forums arguing with each other, articles contradicting each other, and enough useless noise to drown out anything actually important if you weren't careful.

I wasn't used to it, like, at all.

So I skimmed.

A lot.

And waited for the Narrative to step in and filter things for me.

It didn't.

Which was how I figured out that it probably only activated when I actually met people in person, instead of just reading about them like some random idiot on the internet.

So, I begrudgingly clicked on links. After seeing how Pritt and Leah navigate this hellscape with ease, I severely underestimated how confusing it can be, and it slowed down my progress severely.

Hours had been lost.

From what I gathered, the ABB only had two Capes.

Which sounded ridiculous on paper when they controlled a fifth of Brockton Bay, especially when compared to the Protectorate's twelve heroes or the Empire's lineup.

But the difference wasn't numbers.

It was what those two could actually do.

Lung, for one, had apparently walked into Brockton Bay and beaten the entire Protectorate team by himself when he first showed up, which immediately made me question why the hell he was listed as a B-lister in whatever system they were using.

Lung didn't even get his own Wiki Page.

Was the Protectorate of Brockton Bay so weak that anyone who steam rolled them was still just considered above average?

The other ABB cape was a teleporting cloner whose most effective fighting method seemed to be infinite terrorist suicides.

Bombing clones and exponential strength? No wonder just two capes can compete with the rest, both of them were incredible force multipliers, and both had abilities that wouldn't actually put their users out of commission if they were hurt on the field.

Lung would simply heal it off, while Oni-Lee's whole thing was not getting caught in the first place.

…ugh, Oni-Lee, something within me seemed to look down at the name in repulsion through sheer principle.

Anyways, the point being that while my ability to gift Familiars were also great force multipliers, and would shoot us up to relevancy extremely quickly, I also now carried the responsibility of my subordinate's wellbeing.

And that brought me right back to the choice in front of me.

I needed sustainability.

I needed someone who could keep my people in both the fight, and from having permanent consequences at doing mistakes.

Which is why I chose Lupusregina Beta.

Someone who could heal others and defend themselves would be invaluable.

The moment the decision settled, the card flickered into existence for a brief second, and with it came the distinct sound of feminine giggling, a playful tone carrying beneath it a sharp sense of sadism.

Then it was gone again, slipping back into that strange space within my soul alongside the others cards.

Just in time.

"You're up," he said, his voice steady as always.

I nodded, pushing myself up from the steel chair, feeling the faint stiffness in my muscles fade almost instantly as I rolled my shoulders back.

I wasn't wearing anything particularly special, just loose pants and that sash Mr. Hong had insisted would help with my image, something about presentation and symbolism that I didn't fully care about but the Oni within me seemed to agree.

And then the mask. Initially I didn't see the point, since I would always be transformed while working as a Cape. But most of my people told me it's more about the image, and also a symbol of what it meant to be a cape.

Looking at it now, I let out a quiet, amused huff.

The lower half of an Oni stared back at me.

I picked it up without hesitation.

"Let's start," I said, stepping past Robert and into the dimly lit hallway beyond.

And then I moved forward.


POV: John Becker
Date: 18th of March – Night

Infiltrating a gang wasn't exactly difficult, especially when it was an unpowered one.

With more capes popping up and deciding that being a villain paid better than anything legal, the number of "goons" had skyrocketed, and that shift had pushed a lot of the older, non-powered gangs into a corner.

The ones that couldn't keep up lost territory fast, their businesses either taken over outright or absorbed under someone stronger who actually had a cape backing them.

That kind of pressure forced unpowered gangs to lower a lot of their recruitment standards, which made it all the easier for Becker to do his job.

There was only ever a real problem when a cape got involved.

Not that it would be Becker's problem at that point.

He was a cop, and a damn good one at that, but he was still just a cop, not PRT. The moment a Parahuman showed up and confirmed their presence, everything got handed over. The case and the fucking headaches, all of it went straight to the PRT, because they were the ones trained to deal with the kind of mess powers tended to bring along.

Becker wasn't trained for that.

And he was fine with it.

He didn't know how to check if he was being messed with by some stranger's hallucination-based ability, and he definitely didn't have the tools to stay calm if something started whispering in his ear that wasn't actually there. That kind of shit was above his pay grade.

What he was good at, though, was slipping into places he wasn't supposed to be and keeping track of the smaller gangs that filled in the cracks of Brockton Bay.

It wasn't what he signed up for when he first enlisted, not even close, but somewhere along the way it turned out he had a knack for it.

Figuring out who mattered and who didn't without drawing attention to himself came naturally after a while.

You spent enough time around the usual spots, picked up on habits, learned where certain people liked to hang out, and after that it was just a matter of being present without being noticeable.

Look busy, look bored, look drunk, whatever the situation called for, and most people would ignore you long enough to say something useful.

The lowered recruitment standards helped too.

All it really took was learning a few rotating code phrases, finding someone with a similar build who kept their face at least partially covered, and taking their place for the night after knocking them out in some alley that no one would bother checking until morning.

Brockton Bay let them cops get away with a lot more looser set of rules than anywhere else he ever worked.

Of course, it never lasted very long, but one night was usually enough.

Besides, this wasn't something Becker did often. The risk was too high to make it a regular move, and the department only called him in for this kind of work when things in Brockton Bay started going from their regular bad to fucking horrifically worse.

Like when Marquis decided it was a good idea to throw hands with the Slaughterhouse Nine, and the body count got high enough that the streets actually seemed to be painted in red. That had been a miserable few weeks, that's for fucking sure.

Or when Lung came in and decided that every Asian gang in the city now belonged to him, bulldozing through years of historical grudges, and cultural divides, all backed by the simple understanding that nobody wanted to be on the receiving end of his attention.

Granted though, it worked.

Fear was a hell of a motivator.

The problem was everything that came after.

Unrest didn't just disappear because someone stronger showed up, it simmered under the surface, waiting for a chance to boil over, and when it did, it made everyone's job harder.

And now this.

For the first time in almost a year, the Empire and the ABB were going at each other openly, just straight violence spilling out into the streets.

All because someone ran over Kaiser's dog.

Becker snorted quietly to himself at the thought.

In his personal opinion, good riddance. Hookwolf was a piece of shit who deserved worse than what he got, and if the world were fair, the guy would have already be rotting in the Birdcage or strapped to a chair waiting for the switch to flip years ago.

Unfortunately, the world wasn't fair.

So professionally, Becker was stuck dealing with the fallout, along with everyone else. The past few weeks had stretched both the police and the PRT thin, constant skirmishes popping up all over the city, each one needing manpower they didn't really have to spare.

Which was how this almost slipped through the cracks.

In the last day alone, a bunch of smaller, unpowered gangs had started reaching out to each other, setting something up behind the scenes, something coordinated enough to raise red flags even with everything else going on.

No one knew what for.

Which was why Becker was here now, standing inside a refurbished warehouse that had been dressed up just enough to pass as something legitimate, blending in as a member of Nine Way High, a small-time smuggling group with connections outside the city.

With practised ease, he had taken 'Tobias' place without much trouble, slipping into the guy's role and joining the rest of the group in their car. No one questioned it, no one even gave him a second look, and before long he was being driven through the quieter parts of Brockton Bay, heading straight for whatever this was supposed to be.

From what he could tell, his "boss" had brought him along as extra muscle, someone to stand around and look intimidating.

Around the large warehouse, the setup was pretty consistent no matter where Becker looked. Most of the lower-ranking members from the different gangs were doing the same thing he was, standing off to the sides with their backs against the walls, arms crossed or hands tucked into pockets, eyes constantly moving as they kept track of everything happening in the room.

Meanwhile, the actual leaders gathered closer to the center, drinks in hand, talking in low voices like this was some kind of business meeting instead of whatever shady shit it actually was.

Everyone was waiting.

"Becker, can you get more information about this?"

The voice came quietly through his earpiece, the small device hidden under the cap and mask he had taken from Tobias.

The setup worked well enough, the mask helping obscure his face while also hiding the microphone, and the tiny camera embedded near the edge gave the people back at HQ at least something to work with, even if the quality wasn't great.

Answering directly would've been stupid, and ratted him out.

Instead, Becker let out a soft huff, shifting his weight like someone who had been standing around too long with nothing to do, before nudging the guy next to him with his elbow.

The man wore the same cap, his build smaller, skin darker, posture loose.

"That was a bad time to zone out," Becker muttered, roughening his voice just enough to sell the act. "The fuck are we doing here again?"

He wasn't worried about getting called out.

As long as you had just enough confidence and didn't hesitate, people tended to assume you belonged. It was one of those weird social things of human behavior that made his job easier than it probably should've been.

Hell, he remembered reading about a couple of guys who walked straight through security carrying a ladder like they owned the place, and nobody stopped them.

Hilarious shit.

The guy he nudged shrugged, not even looking that concerned. "Dunno, Davos didn't say shit."

Not that surprising, OpSec was a basic necessity to events like this. Which meant the only people who actually knew what was going on were the ones standing in the middle right now.

from where he stood, Becker could pick out at least four different small-time bosses he recognized, each one keeping their distance while still playing nice.

"Davos never says shit," Becker replied flatly, letting a bit of annoyance bleed into his tone.

On his other side, a blond guy let out a short laugh, clearly more entertained by the whole situation, "Well, I heard this whole thing's happening because of the ABB."

Of fucking course it was.

Becker swallowed the instinctive groan that tried to come up and turned his head just enough to face him, angling it so the mic would catch everything clearly.

"The ABB?"

"You didn't hear it from me," the blond continued, leaning in slightly, "but apparently a few days ago, Lung sent in a dozen guys to try and bring Night's Rest under his territory."

"The fuck?" the first guy blurted out in shock.

Becker felt the same.

Night's Rest wasn't exactly unknown to the authorities. They knew what it was, or at least the outline of it, a neutral ground where smaller gangs could exist without constantly getting dragged into conflicts.

There were rumors too.

Talk about parahumans, about the owner maybe being one, but nothing solid enough to act on.

There hasn't been any conflicts, or attempts to attack these elements in Night's Rest, because if the Police were to do such thing, the neutral zone wouldn't be 'safe' anymore for them, and it would definitely cause the small gangs to either scatter to the wind, or join up with the cape one's, something they definitely didn't need.

For better or worse, Night's Rest existed as a stabilizing influence for the unpowered gangs.

"What did he want?" the blond asked, and Becker had to resist the urge to roll his eyes.

What did gangs ever want?

"They wanted the place," the guy replied, gesturing vaguely. "Like, the whole neutral zone. Would make checkpoints and border skirmishes easier, y'know?"

No, Becker didn't know that.

But him being here was definitely paying off.

Through the earpiece, he could faintly hear movement picking up, voices overlapping as people on the other end scrambled to pass the information along, probably feeding it straight to analysts or updating reports in real time.

"Shit," the guy to his right muttered.

Yeah.

Shit was right.

If the ABB got control of that area, it would open up direct access routes into both Protectorate-controlled zones and Coil's territory.

That shit will lead to even more escalating clashes, fuck.

"I know, right?" the blond said, though his tone didn't match the seriousness of it at all. If anything, he sounded excited. "But here's the funny part. Apparently, they all ran away like bitches."

"What?" Becker blurted before he could stop himself, the word slipping out sharp.

Thankfully the others didn't comment, seeing it only as surprise.

"Yeah," the blond went on, grinning slightly. "They walked in, and then next thing you know, they're running out of the bar like something's chasing them."

"How?" Becker asked, trying to keep his tone balanced. He didn't need to hear the analysts to know they wanted that answer.

The blond shrugged, like it wasn't that big of a deal. "Probably the Iron Bull."

Right, that was the Robert guy, isn't it? The owner of Night's Rest. Ex-military, and from what he remembers vaguely, had been charged with a few arrests before they all fell through for some reason or another.

Suspected Parahuman as well, though those searches lead nowhere.

The conversation around him died off before it could really go anywhere, Becker feeling as the attention of the room shifted towards the stage, as a figure stepped up into the light.

His eye narrowed. The man on the stage was dressed like some kind of monk, with various layers of clothing, and had half of his face hidden beneath a veil to conceal his features.

Spoiler: Xiou Hong: Monk Style

The reaction from the crowd was immediate, confusion rippling through the gathered gang members as people tried to figure out what the hell a cape was doing here.

Becker on the other hand, could feel the fucking headaches coming his way.

Yeah, I know!

No shirt, just loose black pants and that sash tied around his waist.

The fuck was a black kid doing running around with that?

He was just a kid, and yet-

His back straightened without meaning to.

It was simple instinct; something about the kid's presence settled over the room like a pressure building before a storm.

Spoiler: Samuel: Oni Style

Shaker? Master effect? The kid was just walking forward at a steady pace, but with every step, it was like further pressure was put on his shoulders.

Black and white unnatural pupils. They caught light with reflectiveness that wasn't normal, like stardust caught in motion, and when they swept across the crowd, Becker felt it land on him for just a second, long enough to make something in his spine lock up before he forced it to relax.

Yeah.

"Most of you are unfamiliar with me," he said, voice steady, carrying easily across the warehouse without needing to be raised, "but as of two days ago, I became the leader of the Neutral Zone."

There was a ripple of shock, unsure looks being traded between people as they shifted and muttered.

Becker kept his face neutral, forced it, even as his mind caught up a second later and he had to lock his jaw to keep from reacting.

"On that same night, the ABB sent out a squad intending to take over the Neutral Zone," The kid said, his voice level and stripped of any emotion. "They failed."

Becker's eyes moved across the gathered crowd, noting the reactions—or rather, the lack of them from certain groups.

Most of the people standing closer to the stage didn't look surprised, the leaders. Either they already knew, or they had been there when it happened, which meant that for them this was less of a speech and more of a continuation of something already in motion.

"As of two days ago, the ABB has shown its fangs and declared war on the Neutral Zone," He continued, his tone unchanged, "They will hunt down and crush every single gang affiliated with the place."

The kid was saying some pretty scary things with a straight face.

Becker believed him, after all, Lung was well known for his brutality.

The words settled over the warehouse like a slow-moving wave, and Becker could feel the shift ripple outward as people processed what that actually meant. Everyone in this place understood exactly what type of man Lung was and what happened when he decided to make an example out of someone.

"However!" His white pupils shone with fire as he cut in, and the single word was enough to halt that downward spiral before it could fully take hold.

"I said it back then, and I will say it again."

…what?

There was no hesitation in the statement, no room for doubt or negotiation.

He said as if it was absolute, like the sun rises and falls, it was such a natural law of the universe that it held the same weight as his words.

"The ABB came to us expecting no resistance," The kid went on, his voice steady as he moved, each step measured as he paced along the stage. "But I will fight them."

There was intent behind the motion when he lifted his foot, something deliberate in the way he set it back down.

"And I will crush them beneath my feet."

The impact echoed.

Becker felt it through the soles of his boots, a sharp tremor that ran through the concrete and up his spine, rattling something deep in his chest even though he knew, rationally, that a single step shouldn't be able to do that.

Enhanced strength? A brute on top of shaker effect?

Around him, people reacted in a split second burst of noise, flinching, swearing, shifting in place before the moment passed and left behind a thick silence that somehow made the air feel tighter.

The kid didn't even pause for it, continuing as if nothing had happened, his pacing controlled while his eyes remained locked on the crowd, holding them there with an intensity Becker couldn't quite shake.

It reminded him of predators.

"Agent Becker? This is PRT responder Travis, I'll be handling your comms."

The voice in his ear barely registered.

Becker didn't respond, couldn't, not without drawing attention, but even if he could have slipped in a reply, he wasn't sure he would have managed it, because his focus stayed pinned to the stage like something had hooked into his brain and refused to let go.

A faint choking sound came through the line a moment later, followed by a rush of hurried voices on the other end, overlapping commands and clipped responses that told him the PRT had just gotten eyes on his camera feed, and were watching the same thing.

"—get me—no, right now—"

Then Travis was back, sharper this time.

"Do not draw attention to yourself! Stay where you are."

They didn't have to worry; he wasn't moving any time soon with such intense pressure weighting him down.

"But that is no longer enough for me," The kid said, shaking his head slightly, the motion somehow coming off as natural. "It is no longer enough for us."

His hand moved outward, gesturing toward the gathered crowd in a way that made it feel less like a speech and more like a claim.

"What is the Neutral Zone?"

The question hung there for half a second before he answered it himself.

"It is a place where violence is forbidden, where people can lower their guard without fearing what waits behind them, where you can live without constantly watching your back."

Becker felt his chest tighten, his pulse picking up in a way he didn't appreciate, because as much as he wanted to stay detached, to keep this clinical and professional, the words still hit somewhere when they shouldn't have.

Because yeah, that was exactly what people wanted.

In a city like Brockton Bay, that kind of place wasn't just appealing, it was everything.

And as much as Becker hated to admit it, standing there in the middle of a warehouse full of criminals, listening to a masked kid declare war on one of the most dangerous gangs in the city, he could feel it too.

That pull.

Because they all wanted to be safe.

"They threaten that safety," he continued, and Becker wondered if their expressions were so easy for him to read.

"They want to see us scattered, and stripped of the only place in this city where we can exist without looking over our shoulders," he went on, and even without raising his volume, the intent behind his words pressed down on everyone listening.

"I will not allow it!"

The conviction in that single sentence hit harder than any shout could have, settling deep in Becker's chest and making his jaw tighten.

"And it doesn't stop there," The kid continued, his gaze sweeping across the crowd, sharp and deliberate, until it passed over Becker for just a fraction of a second, and that brief contact made his pulse hitch under the intensity behind those eyes.

"For how long have you all been living under that dragon's shadow? How long have woman been too afraid to walk their own streets without wondering if they'll ever make it back home?"

Becker became aware of his teeth grinding together, a slow burn of anger rising up as the words dug into a familiar issue, dragging up every report, every scene, every case that had crossed his desk involving the ABB.

"It doesn't stop there," His white eyes shined as he pushed on, his voice rising now, "What about the ones who don't fit into someone else's idea of normal, forced to watch over their every step in fear of being targeted? What about the kids getting dragged off the streets and turned into addicts before they even know what's happening? What about the people paying protection money just to avoid having their doors kicked in?"

This kid…

The realization settled in Becker's mind, because this wasn't just a speech about the ABB anymore, it was broader than that, aimed at every major player in Brockton Bay, dragging all of it into the open without hesitation.

The kid was stoking their anger.

And he's really fucking good at it.

"I will see this city rid of that," The fire was still there as he spoke, and his tone shifted again, calmer now, "A place of safety, that is what the Neutral Zone was meant to be, and that is what it will remain."

He didn't pause, didn't give the crowd time to interrupt or question, just kept moving forward with the same absolute certainty that somehow grabbed the to their very souls.

"Anyone who threatens that safety will be crushed beneath me."

It was arrogant, in fact, it was even beyond that. The size of the ambition, the absolute certainty of which he spoke of himself with.

This kid was claiming that he would accomplish what the Protectorate and the PRT haven't been able to in the last ten years.

"Most of you doubt me," He added, his voice even, likely already accounting for every sceptical thought in the room.

However, Becker found himself staring without blinking, a tight anticipation coiling in his gut as if something was about to snap into place.

And then it did.

His body began to change.

His frame grew, stretching upward and pushing past what should have been possible, until where previously the kid had stood, was now a figure measuring three meters, the structure beneath him creaking under the sudden increase in mass.

His dark skin gained color, except it was wrong, a deep red like blood coming to the surface, barely catching the dim light of the warehouse, while a pair of horns curved up from his head, completely the transformation into what was undoubtably a Monster.

Spoiler: "The Demon"

It wasn't just his looks either, Becker thought as his body shook.

The pressure that had been sitting in the background since the moment the kid walked in suddenly surged outward, no longer restrained, flooding the room in a way that made it feel like the air itself had thickened, pressing against his lungs as if daring him to keep breathing.

Power.

Overwhelming.

"I am Ura-Douji," the transformed figure declared, his voice deeper now, carrying a force that seemed to vibrate through the space itself.

"The Demon of the Bay."

It was a simple title, something that should have sounded dramatic at best, maybe even ridiculous under different circumstances, but standing there, feeling that crushing weight settle over him, Becker knew with a certainty that bypassed logic entirely that this wasn't posturing.

Before him stood a Demon.

'Ura-Douji?!' Travis' voice spiked in his ear, and Becker felt the same realization click into place a second later.

That cape's name. They didn't even know if that cape truly existed, since their only information was a second-hand account of rumours spread by the Empire, and a crushed, mangled corpse that used to be Hookwolf

There had been questions of whether the cape simply left Brockton after two weeks of inactivity, or if the battle with Hookwolf damaged them enough that they were still recovering.

Well, that question just got answered.

"With my Power! We are the Strongest!" Ura-Douji proclaimed, his voice carrying across the warehouse as his fists clenched, the motion so forceful that the air itself cracked.

Then, as if on cue, four figures moved to his side.

The monk stepped forward again, calm and composed beneath that concealing veil, while the others emerged from the edges of the stage, each one masked.

Beckers gaze flicked between them, taking in what he could and shifting his camera so that they could as well.

The first was what he would call, 'skimpy biker girl' with the way the coat didn't even cover her mid drift, and how small her shorts were. Thought her helmet did seem to be of good quality.

Spoiler: Kelly: Biker Style

The other one seemed to emanate danger, his entire body was covered head from head to toe in some type of mix between a normal cape spandex look, and a few pieces of body armour.

Spoiler: Bryce: Shield Style

The last guy, compared to the previous two, was much more boring in terms of outfit. Wearing a simple tight-fitting shirt that made Becker feel inadequate, the army style was obvious.

Spoiler: Robert: Mlitary Style

Capes, he knew they were fucking capes.

How? How the fuck did a no name somehow already have capes under him, how did he pull this off without anyone noticing?

The answer came a second later.

They transformed.

The monk was the first, a soft pink aura blooming around him as a microphone appeared in his hand, the object spinning once before settling as if it belonged there, his once brown clothing now transforming into a black robe with golden highlights-
Spoiler: Lullaby

The others followed, and Becker felt his breath hitch as skin turned to stone, bodies expanding, wings of rock tearing free from their backs while rocky tails formed behind them-

Spoiler: Furnace + Tempest

And the last one—

The last one became iron.

Literally, from his clothes to his skin and even his eyes, everything just straight up became iron.

Spoiler: Bull Iron

Becker's mind scrambled to keep up, piecing it together as his eyes snapped back to Ura-Douji, to the towering figure that had already changed once in front of them, they all wore black and gold, their gang colours? How ostentatious.

And they were all-

Transformations.

All of them… just like the kid….

His previous words, his power that made him the strongest.

"Oh… oh fuck," Becker muttered under his breath, barely aware of it.

'Oh fuck,' Travis echoed in his ear, the words coming out strained.

It's a Teacher situation.

Not the same, not exactly—but close enough to make every alarm bell in his head start screaming. A power that granted powers, something that could turn normal people into monstrous capes, something that could build a terrifying gang if left unchecked.

No, is in the fucking process of building one!

A Power Giving Trump.

"With my strength! I shall carve a path forward, but with the strength of my allies, the path shall become a road for all those who will follow!" Ura-Douji continued, his voice rising, filling the space with something that made Becker's chest tighten as he listened.

His heart was hammering.

"Those who will fight for this cause, those who are willing to bleed for a Brockton Bay without the tyranny of the dragon, or chains of outdated beliefs!" The crowd was reacting now, and Becker could feel himself getting pulled along with them, a strange warmth coiling in his chest, something that made his hands clench.

"I am the Demon of the Bay, Ura-Douji!" The King bellows, before expanding his arms like he was trying to hug the entire crowd of hundreds, "And those who have put their faith in me, shall become my Night Parade of One Hundred Demons!"

The crowd's response was as instant as it was overwhelmingly loud.

Cheer erupted around him, voices rising into a roar that filled the warehouse, and before Becker could stop himself, he found his own voice joining in, the sound ripping out of his throat as the two men beside him jumped and shouted like they'd just been handed salvation.

However, the words soon caught up to him.

A Hundred Demons? The kid could give powers to a hundred people?

Insane, absolutely fucking insane.

His pulse pounded in his ears, every beat syncing with the energy in the room, every breath coming faster as something electric ran through him.

"Dispatch," Becker muttered under his breath, low enough that the noise swallowed it, his tone tight despite the chaos around him, "I think there is a Master effect at play. I've been compromised."

Because there was no way this was normal.

The way his chest felt like it was going to burst, the rush that came with hearing someone promise protection and actually sound like they could deliver it, the sudden, burning urge to stand behind that man and see it through—

That had to be artificial.

It had to be.

Because if it wasn't…

Becker stared at the towering figure on the stage, at the Demon who had with simple words, won over a large portion of the city.

If it wasn't—

Then he didn't know if the Protectorate, or anyone else in Brockton Bay, had a chance in hell of stopping him.

POV: Samuel Rodrigues

DATE: 18th​ of March – Night


[FEAT: Gain the unbridled animosity of the Dragon and the ABB

1x Random Gold Gacha Ticket]

1x Random Silver Gacha Ticket]

1x Random Platinum Gacha Ticket]


[FEAT: Give Hope to the Hopeless

1x Random Diamond Gacha Ticket]

​

Well, at least it was a productive rally then.

By the time everything wrapped up, I had already made my way back to Night's Rest. It felt quieter than usual after the sheer noise and pressure of the warehouse, the lingering energy of what I had just done still buzzed faintly under my skin. The leaders of the smaller gangs had agreed to meet again soon, and one by one they had taken the drink I offered, stepping into my service with a mix of hesitation and anticipation.

I leaned back slightly in my chair, thinking over the next steps, already knowing that I'd have to figure out how to actually reward them in a way that was tangible enough to keep loyalty strong without wasting resources I might need later.

Right now, the easiest option that came to mind was handing out Junimos to the bulk of their henchmen, since even if their powers weren't flashy, they were useful in ways that would stack up fast if applied correctly.

Originally, I could call up to fifty, but after growing in strength with every pull, I can call about one hundred and seventy of them now.

Their druid-like abilities alone would be enough to make them valuable for growing… less-than-legal crops, and the minor boost to speed wasn't anything to scoff at. It would put a normal guy straight into Olympic levels. They probably wouldn't win, but that's already impressive.

But what really made them dangerous was their ability to phase through walls, which turned something simple into a logistical nightmare for anyone on the receiving end. The idea of having dozens of people who completely invalidated any type of physical barrier would shift things heavily in our favor.

I did promise power, after all.

And judging by how the crowd reacted, almost everyone there had taken that promise seriously, scrambling to get in line and secure a place before I changed my mind. Even if they had hesitated to place themselves beneath a kid, the possibility of getting powers changed their mind quickly.

It had been exhausting dealing with all of that, talking more than I ever had in one sitting, all while leaning into a vague inspiring tone that Leadership insists was necessary for these kinds of gatherings.

Eventually, I cut it short and told the important ones to regroup here so we could actually discuss our strategy plan, and how things were gonna roll from now on.

They should be here in about ten minutes.

Which left me with nothing to do but watch the clock tick forward.

…

I rubbed at the side of my face for a second.

At least this one comes with actual, useful effects instead of just making certain people more attracted to me.

If anything, having my usual attitude come across as charming instead of disrespectful will save me a lot of trouble down the line when dealing with people who had enough power to make things annoying if they feel slighted.

Gambler, don't screw me over again. Let's try a normal Gold.
